Metrical properties of Hurwitz Continued Fractions
Yann Bugeaud, Gerardo González Robert, Mumtaz Hussain

Abstract
. We develop the geometry of Hurwitz continued fractions, a major tool in understanding the approximation properties of complex numbers by ratios of Gaussian integers. Based on a thorough study of the geometric properties of Hurwitz continued fractions, among other things, we determine that the space of valid sequences is not a closed set of sequences. Additionally, we establish a comprehensive metrical theory for Hurwitz continued fractions. Let Φ : ℕ → ℝ > 0 \Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0} be any function. For any complex number z z and n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} , let a n ​ ( z ) a_{n}(z) denote the n n th partial quotient in the Hurwitz continued fraction of z z . One of the main results of this paper is the computation of the Hausdorff dimension of the set E ⁡ ( Φ ) := { z ∈ ℂ : | a n ​ ( z ) | ≥ Φ ⁡ ( n ) ​ for infinitely many ​ n ∈ ℕ } . E(\Phi)\colon=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|a_{n}(z)|\geq\Phi(n)\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}. This study is a complex analog of a well-known result of Wang and Wu [Adv. Math. 218 (2008), no. 5, 1319–1339].



1.
Introduction

A classical result states that for each real number
x
there is a sequence
(A_{n}(x))_{n\geq 0}
of integers, where
A_{n}(x)\geq 1
for
n\geq 1
, such that

x=A_{0}(x)+\cfrac{1}{A_{1}(x)+\cfrac{1}{A_{2}(x)+\cfrac{1}{\ddots}}}.

Such expansion, which is infinite if and only if
x
is an irrational number, is known as the regular (real) continued fraction of
x
.
Regular continued fractions are a fundamental tool for understanding irrational numbers, partly due to their ability to provide the best rational approximations of real numbers
[
9
]
. However, their applications go beyond this property. They enable us to draw connections with various branches of mathematics such as dynamical systems, ergodic theory, and fractal geometry, thus unveiling shared characteristics among different classes of irrational numbers

[
3
,
11
,
20
,
22
,
49
,
56
]
.
We refer the reader to
[
41
]
for the classical theory of regular continued fractions and to
[
7
]
for a modern account.

The Borel-Bernstein Theorem (see, for example,
[
7
, Theorem 1.11]
) and similar results provide us with families of null sets (Lebesgue measure zero) determined by their continued fraction expansions and, hence, by their approximation properties. A classic example is the set of badly approximable numbers; this is precisely the set of real numbers with bounded partial quotients. Hausdorff and packing dimensions have been used to determine the size of such sets. The research in this vein dates back to Jarník
[
39
]
, who proved that the set of badly approximable numbers has full Hausdorff dimension. Good’s celebrated work
[
27
]
concerns the size of small null sets obtained by imposing asymptotic conditions on the partial quotients (for refinements and extensions, see
[
47
,
55
]
). The Hausdorff dimension of the null sets appearing in the Borel-Bernstein Theorem was calculated by Wang and Wu in their seminal paper
[
55
]
.
Other sets related to Diophantine approximation, such as the Markov and Lagrange spectrum, have also been studied with the aid of regular continued fractions
[
48
]
.

Regular continued fractions link analytic and algebraic properties of irrational numbers. Among the classical results in this direction, we have Lagrange’s Theorem on quadratic surds
[
41
, Theorem 28]
or Liouville’s construction of transcendental numbers, where transcendence is obtained by ensuring good rational approximation
[
41
, Section 9]
. More recently, Adamczewski and Bugeaud used Schmidt’s Subspace Theorem to build families of badly approximable transcendental numbers
[
1
,
8
]
.

Extending the theory of continued fraction expansions to higher dimensions while preserving most of the aforementioned properties is, thus, highly desirable. However, such extensions are far from straightforward and may not always be possible. In this paper, we focus on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
. In
\mathbb{C}
, several continued fraction algorithms have been suggested depending on the preferred properties
[
12
,
34
,
36
,
45
,
50
,
51
]
. In all of these expansions, the resulting space of sequences is a major concern. However, Shallit’s work
[
51
]
completely describes the sequence space of his algorithm using automata theory.
In
[
36
]
, Adolf Hurwitz introduced a complex continued fraction algorithm.
Hurwitz continued fractions (defined in Section
4
) give a continued fraction expansion for complex numbers that share several helpful properties with regular continued fractions (see, for example, propositions
4.1
and
4.2
).
In particular, they provide good Gaussian rational approximations to a given complex number. In
[
44
]
, Lakein relied on these approximation properties to obtain a new proof of Ford’s Theorem on approximation to complex numbers by elements of
\mathbb{Q}(i)
. A word of caution: while drawing an analogy between Hurwitz and the nearest-integer continued fraction algorithm may seem more natural, we choose to focus on the more widely studied regular continued fractions.

The theory of Hurwitz continued fractions has recently been used in complex Diophantine approximation, yet it is far from being as well understood as their real counterpart.
In
[
14
, Section 7]
, Dani and Nogueira extended their work
[
13
]
on real binary quadratic forms to complex numbers using Hurwitz continued fractions. In
[
53
]
, Simmons studied Diophantine approximation properties of Hurwitz continued fractions when restricted to real numbers by comparing them with regular continued fractions.
Hines
[
33
]
and González Robert
[
26
]
showed independently that badly approximable complex numbers have bounded Hurwitz continued fraction partial quotients. Hines’ work, however, includes other quadratic imaginary fields. There are some (rather surprising) results on the partial quotients of complex algebraic numbers. Hensley
[
31
]
was the first to find examples of algebraic complex numbers of degree four over
\mathbb{Q}(i)
and with bounded complex partial quotients. This was subsequently generalized by Bosma and Gruenewald
[
5
]
, who showed that, for every even integer
d
, there exist algebraic complex non-real numbers of degree
d
over
\mathbb{Q}
for which the Hurwitz continued fraction expansion has bounded partial quotients. In
[
26
]
, González Robert constructed a family of badly approximable transcendental complex numbers giving a partial analog of
[
8
]
.
In
[
30
]
, He and Xiong calculated the Hausdorff dimension of sets of complex numbers that can be approximated by quotients of Gaussian integers at a given order
\psi
but no better. This is a complex version of a problem solved by Bugeaud
[
6
]
. Lastly, the theory developed in this paper was applied by García-Ramos, González Robert, and Hussain to study topological, dynamical, and descriptive set-theoretic properties of Hurwitz continued fractions
[
23
]
. Some of the applications in their paper include determining the rank in the Borel hierarchy of the set of Hurwitz normal numbers with respect to the complex Gauss measure and the construction of a family of
complex transcendental numbers with bounded partial quotients.

In this paper, we compute the Hausdorff dimension of sets of complex numbers whose Hurwitz continued fraction has a subsequence of partial quotients of fast growth. Consequently, we obtain a complex analog of the Borel-Bernstein refinement by Wang-Wu
[
55
]
.
A significant part of our work is dedicated to studying two intimately related aspects of Hurwitz continued fractions: their geometric properties and their symbolic space. In particular, we show that the space of sequences,
the partial quotients in the Hurwitz continued fraction of a complex number, is not a closed subset of the space of sequences of Gaussian integers
\mathbb{Z}[i]^{\mathbb{N}}
(see the proof of Lemma
6.5
). However, we can replace it with the closure of a subset
\mathsf{R}
of
\mathbb{Z}[i]^{\mathbb{N}}
. The set
\mathsf{R}
will be defined in simple topological terms in Section
4
.

Let us state our main results.
For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\lfloor t\rfloor
be the integer part of
t
, that is

\lfloor t\rfloor\colon=\max\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:m\leq t\}.

For each
z\in\mathbb{C}
, define the nearest Gaussian integer
[z]
to
z
as

[z]\colon=\left\lfloor\real(z)+\frac{1}{2}\right\rfloor+i\left\lfloor\imag(z)+\frac{1}{2}\right\rfloor.

Define the set

\mathfrak{F}\colon=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:[z]=0\}.

We will restrict our attention to
\mathfrak{F}
.
Note that
\mathfrak{F}
is the unit square centered at the origin whose sides are parallel to the coordinate axes, it includes the left and the bottom sides and excludes the remaining two. Also, the Gaussian integral translates of
\mathfrak{F}
form a partition of
\mathbb{C}
.
The rigorous definitions of Hurwitz continued fractions and some related terms are fully explained in Section
4
. For now, we just need to know that the Hurwitz continued fraction process associates to each complex number
z
a (finite or infinite) sequence
(a_{n}(z))_{n\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{Z}[i]
such that

(1)

z=[a_{0}(z);a_{1}(z),a_{2}(z),\ldots]=a_{0}(z)+\cfrac{1}{a_{1}(z)+\cfrac{1}{a_{2}(z)+\cfrac{1}{\ddots}}}.

If
z\in\mathfrak{F}
, then
a_{0}(z)=0
and we denote the continued fraction in (
1
) by
[a_{1}(z),a_{2}(z),\ldots]
or simply
[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]
. For any
B>1
, we write

(2)

s_{B}\colon=\lim_{n\to\infty}\inf\left\{\rho\geq 0:\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{1}{B^{n}|q_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|^{2}}\right)^{\rho}<1\right\}.

Let
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(A)
denote the Hausdorff dimension of a given subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, we refer the reader to
[
18
]
for an account of the theory of Hausdorff dimension.
Write
\|z\|\colon=\max\{|\real(z)|,|\imag(z)|\}
for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any function
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, define the set

E_{\infty}(\Phi)\colon=\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq\Phi(n)\text{ \rm for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

and
B\geq 1
by

\log B=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\Phi(n)}{n}.

The Hausdorff dimension of
E_{\infty}(\Phi)
is as follows:

i.

If
B=1
, then
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))=2
.

ii.

If
1<B<\infty
, then
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))=s_{B}
.

iii.

When
B=\infty
, let
b
be given by

\log b=\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\log\Phi(n)}{n}.

Then, we have

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))=\frac{2}{1+b}.

Remark 1.2
.

Theorem
1.1
holds if we replace
\|\cdot\|
with the complex absolute value
|\cdot|
. Indeed, for every
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
c>0
, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\Phi(n)}{n}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log c\,\Phi(n)}{n},

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\log\Phi(n)}{n}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\log c\,\Phi(n)}{n}.

Then, by Theorem
1.1
,
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))=\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(c\Phi))
. Putting

E(\Phi)\colon=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|a_{n}(z)|\geq\Phi(n)\text{ \rm for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

we have

E_{\infty}\left(2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi\right)\subseteq E(\Phi)\subseteq E_{\infty}(\Phi),

and, thus,

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E(\Phi))=\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi)).

A crucial step towards proving Theorem
1.1
is the computation of the Hausdorff dimension of the sets
F(B)
for
B>1
, defined as

F(B)\colon=\{z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq B^{n}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Theorem 1.3
.

For each
1<B<\infty
, we have
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F(B))=s_{B}
.

In
[
47
]
, Łuczak calculated the Hausdorff dimension of sets of real numbers with fastly growing partial quotients. Given
x\in(0,1)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, let
[A_{1}(x),A_{2}(x),\ldots]
denote its regular continued fraction. For
b,c>1
, define the sets

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}_{\mathbb{R}}(b,c)

\displaystyle\colon=\{x\in(0,1):c^{b^{n}}\leq A_{n}(x)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\},

\displaystyle\Xi_{\mathbb{R}}(b,c)

\displaystyle\colon=\{x\in(0,1):c^{b^{n}}\leq A_{n}(x)\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Theorem 1.4

(Łuczak,
[
47
]
)
.

For every pair of real numbers
b,c>1
, we have

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\widetilde{\Xi}_{\mathbb{R}}(b,c)\right)=\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\Xi_{\mathbb{R}}(b,c)\right)=\frac{1}{1+b}.

The case
B=\infty
in Theorem
1.1
requires a complex version of Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 1.5
.

If
b,c>1
, then

\displaystyle\frac{2}{1+b}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq|a_{n}(z)|\,\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq|a_{n}(z)|\,\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Theorem
1.5
provides an alternative proof of the lower bound in Good’s theorem for Hurwitz continued fractions
[
25
, Theorem 1.3]
; that is,

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\lim_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}(z)|=\infty\right\}\right)=1.

Certainly, note that for any pair of real numbers
b>1
and
c>1
, we have

\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq|a_{n}(z)|\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subseteq\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\lim_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}(z)|=\infty\right\}.

Theorem
1.5
implies that

\frac{2}{1+b}\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\lim_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}(z)|=\infty\right\}\right)

and letting
b\to 1
we conclude that

1\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\lim_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}(z)|=\infty\right\}\right).

The upper bound can be shown with a natural covering argument.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
contains a summary of notation.
In Section
3
, we establish some terminology concerning Gaussian-integral translations and the complex inversion.
In Section
4
, we define Hurwitz continued fractions and recall some of their basic properties; in particular, we define various types of cylinders (regular, irregular, extremely irregular, full, and almost full).
In Sections
5
and
6
, we study the geometric properties of Hurwitz continued fractions.
More precisely, in Section
5
, we discuss the symmetries and the size of regular cylinders. In Section
6
, we estimate how many regular cylinders of a given level are intersected by small discs. These estimate will be used for the lower bound in Theorem
1.3
. The detailed explanation of the geometric aspects of Hurwitz continued fractions is of independent interest.
In Section
7
, we discuss how we can approximate irregular numbers by regular numbers (defined in Section
4
).
Sections
8
and
9
are dedicated to the proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
, respectively.
Section
10
contains the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Lastly, in
11
, we give some final remarks and open problems.

Estancias posdoctorales por México
of the CONACyT, Mexico. We thank the anonymous referees for useful comments and suggestions that led to significant improvements.

2.
Frequently used notation

For convenience, we collect some of the frequently used notation. The vast majority of the symbols, however, are defined throughout the text.

1.

If
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are two sequences of positive numbers, we write
x_{n}\ll y_{n}
if there is some constant
c>0
such that
x_{n}\leq cy_{n}
for all large
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
x_{n}\asymp y_{n}
if
x_{n}\ll y_{n}
and
y_{n}\ll x_{n}
.

2.

\overline{\mathbb{C}}
stands for the Riemann sphere.

3.

We define
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
by
\iota(z)=z^{-1}
and, for
a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
,
\tau_{a}:\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
by
\tau_{a}(z)=z+a
. We consider the usual conventions for
z=0
and
z=\infty
. The composition of these functions is denoted by concatenation.

4.

Put
\alpha:=\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{2}
and
\zeta_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}+i\alpha
.

5.

For all
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
, write

[z,w):=\{z+t(w-z):t\in[0,1)\},\quad[z,w]:=\{z+t(w-z):t\in[0,1]\},

(z,w):=\{z+t(w-z):t\in(0,1)\},\quad(z,w]:=\{z+t(w-z):t\in(0,1]\}.

6.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
, the functions
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
,
T_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a}):\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
are given by

T_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})\colon=\tau_{-a_{n}}\iota\cdots\tau_{-a_{1}}\iota,\quad Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})\colon=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{n}}.

7.

For every
A\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, we denote the closure of
A
by
\Cl(A)
, the interior of
A
by
\inte(A)
or
A^{\circ}
, and the boundary of
A
by
\Fr(A)
.

8.

We denote the empty word by
\epsilon
.

9.

If
X
is a non-empty finite set,
\#X
denotes the number of elements contained in
X
.

10.

If
X
,
Y
are non-empty sets,
A\subseteq X
, and
f:X\to Y
is any function, the image of
A
under
f
is
f[A]
; that is,
f[A]\colon=\{f(a):a\in A\}
.

11.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, write
\|z\|\colon=\max\{|\real(z)|,|\imag(z)|\}
.

12.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, write
\Pm(z)\colon=\min\{|\real(z)|,|\imag(z)|\}
.

13.

\mathscr{D}\colon=\{a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]:|a|\geq\sqrt{2}\}
.

14.

\mathscr{E}\colon=\{a\in\mathscr{D}:|a|\geq\sqrt{8}\}
.

15.

For
M>0
, we define
\mathscr{D}(M)\colon=\{a\in\mathscr{D}:\|a\|\leq M\}
.

16.

The functions
\mathsf{rot},\mathsf{mir}_{1},\mathsf{mir}_{2}:\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
are given by

\mathsf{rot}(z)=iz,\quad\mathsf{mir}_{1}(z)=\overline{z},\quad\text{and }\quad\mathsf{mir}_{2}(z)=-\overline{z}\quad\text{ for all }z\in\mathbb{C}

and
\mathsf{rot}(\infty)=\mathsf{mir}_{1}(\infty)=\mathsf{mir}_{2}(\infty)=\infty
.

17.

The dihedral group of order
8
, which is generated by
\mathsf{rot}
and
\mathsf{mir}_{1}
, is denoted by
\mathsf{Di}_{8}
. The subgroup of
\mathsf{Di}_{8}
generated by
\mathsf{rot}
is denoted by
\mathsf{Rot}
.

18.

The orbit of
\zeta_{1}
under the action of
\mathsf{Rot}
is denoted by
\mathsf{Rot}(\zeta_{1})
and similarly for
\mathsf{Di}_{8}
; that is,
\mathsf{Rot}(\zeta_{1})=\{f(\zeta_{1}):f\in\mathsf{Rot}\}
and
\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})=\{f(\zeta_{1}):f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}\}
.

19.

For any two sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, we write
A\equiv B\mod\mathsf{Rot}
(resp.
A\equiv B\mod\mathsf{Di}_{8}
) if and only if there is some
f\in\mathsf{Rot}
(resp.
f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
) such that
A=f[B]
.

20.

The distance between two non-empty sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is

\dist(A,B)\colon=\inf\{|a-b|:a\in A,b\in B\}.

21.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
and any
\rho>0
, we write

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho)\colon=\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|z-w|<\rho\},\quad\overline{\mathbb{D}}(z;\rho)\colon=\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|z-w|\leq\rho\},

and

C(z,\rho)\colon=\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|z-w|=\rho\}.

Following
[
43
]
, we write

\mathbb{D}(z)\colon=\mathbb{D}(z;1),\quad\overline{\mathbb{D}}(z)\colon=\overline{\mathbb{D}}(z;1).

22.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathbb{N}}
, the
k
-th
prefix
of
\mathbf{a}
is
\prefi(\mathbf{a};k)\colon=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
.

23.

If
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
, and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\in\mathscr{D}^{m}
, we write

\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\colon=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\in\mathscr{D}^{n+m}.

24.

If
\mathbf{a}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence and
u,v
are natural numbers such that
u<v
, we write
\mathbf{a}[u,v]=(a_{u},a_{u+1},\ldots,a_{v})
.

3.
Translations and the complex inversion

In this section, we set up the notation for the complex inversion and a family of translations which will be used throughout this paper.
Let
\overline{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
denote the Riemann sphere. The
inversion

\iota:\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
is the map given by

\forall z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\quad\quad\iota(z)\colon=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }z=\infty,\\
\infty,&\text{ if }z=0,\\
\frac{1}{z},&\text{ if }z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0,\infty\}.\end{cases}

For any
a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
, the
translation

\tau_{a}:\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
is given by

\forall z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\quad\quad\tau_{a}(z)\colon=\begin{cases}z+a,&\text{ if }z\in\mathbb{C},\\
\infty&\text{ if }z=\infty.\end{cases}

We represent the composition of these functions by juxtaposing them; for instance,
\tau_{a}\iota:=\tau_{a}\circ\iota
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
.

Remark 3.1
.

The set
\mathfrak{F}
is a fundamental domain for the action of
\{\tau_{a}:a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]\}
on
\mathbb{C}
. Then, the nearest Gaussian integer
[z]
of a given
z\in\mathbb{C}
is the only number
a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
satisfying one of the equivalent conditions:

i.

z=\tau_{a}(w)
for some
w\in\mathfrak{F}
,

ii.

\tau_{-a}(z)\in\mathfrak{F}
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[i]^{n}
, define
T_{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{a}):\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
by

\forall z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\quad T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a})\colon=\tau_{-a_{n}}\iota\cdots\tau_{-a_{1}}\iota(z)\;\text{ and }\;Q_{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{a})\colon=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{n}}(z).

Clearly,
T_{n}(\cdot,\mathbf{a})
and
Q_{n}(\cdot,\mathbf{a})
are mutually inverse homeomorphisms from
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
onto itself. For any set
A\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{C}}
, we denote its closure by
\Cl(A)
, its interior by
\inte(A)
and its image under
T_{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{a})
and
Q_{n}(\cdot;\mathbf{a})
, respectively, by
T_{n}(A;\mathbf{a})
and
Q_{n}(A;\mathbf{a})
; that is,

T_{n}(A;\mathbf{a}):=\left\{T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a}):z\in A\right\}\;\text{ and }\;Q_{n}(A;\mathbf{a}):=\left\{Q_{n}(z;\mathbf{a}):z\in A\right\}.

Observe that

\displaystyle\Cl(T_{n}(A;\mathbf{a}))=T_{n}(\Cl(A);\mathbf{a}),

\displaystyle\quad\inte(T_{n}(A;\mathbf{a}))=T_{n}(\inte(A);\mathbf{a}),

\displaystyle\Cl(Q_{n}(A;\mathbf{a}))=Q_{n}(\Cl(A);\mathbf{a}),

\displaystyle\quad\inte(Q_{n}(A;\mathbf{a}))=Q_{n}(\inte(A);\mathbf{a}).

We will use these four identities without further reference.

Lastly, we recall some well-known formulas concerning the action of
\iota
on circles in the Riemann sphere.
For
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\rho>0
, write

C(z;\rho):=\left\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|z-w|=\rho\right\}.

First, if
k\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, then

(3)

\iota\left[\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\real(z)=k\}\right]=C\left(\frac{1}{2k};\frac{1}{2|k|}\right),\quad\iota\left[\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\imag(z)=k\}\right]=C\left(\frac{-i}{2k};\frac{1}{2|k|}\right).

These equations are implicitly used throughout the paper.
If
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\rho>0
are such that
\rho\neq|z_{0}|
, then

(4)

\iota\left[C(z_{0};\rho)\right]=C\left(\frac{\overline{z_{0}}}{|z|^{2}-\rho^{2}};\frac{\rho}{|\rho^{2}-|z_{0}|^{2}|}\right).

4.
Basics on Hurwitz continued fractions

In this section, we recall the definition of Hurwitz continued fractions and some of their basic properties. We define Hurwitz continued fractions in two steps: first, we restrict ourselves to numbers in
\mathfrak{F}\setminus\{0\}
and, then, consider numbers in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Let the
Hurwitz-Gauss map

T:\mathfrak{F}\to\mathfrak{F}
be given by

\forall z\in\mathfrak{F}\quad\quad T(z)=\begin{cases}z^{-1}-[z^{-1}],&\text{ if }z\neq 0,\\
0,&\text{ if }z=0.\end{cases}

For any
z\in\mathfrak{F}\setminus\{0\}
, define
a_{0}(z)\colon=0
,
a_{1}(z)\colon=[z^{-1}]
and
a_{n}(z)\colon=a_{1}(T^{n-1}(z))
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which
T^{n-1}(z)\neq 0
. Naturally, the exponent in
T
denotes iteration. For
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathfrak{F}
, we define
a_{0}(z)=[z]
and
a_{n}(z)=a_{n}(z-[z])
whenever the right-hand side is defined. The
Hurwitz continued fraction
(Hcf) of
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
is the expression

[a_{0}(z);a_{1}(z),a_{2}(z),\ldots]\colon=a_{0}(z)+\cfrac{1}{a_{1}(z)+\cfrac{1}{a_{2}(z)+\cfrac{1}{\ddots}}}.

As mentioned in the introduction, when
z\in\mathfrak{F}
, we have
a_{0}(z)=0
and we write
[a_{1}(z),a_{2}(z),\ldots]
(resp.
[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]
) rather than
[0;a_{1}(z),a_{2}(z),\ldots]
(resp.
[0;a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]
). As usual, we can interpret the previous expression as a limit (see Proposition
4.1
.
iii
). The terms of the sequence
(a_{n}(z))_{n\geq 1}
are called the
Hurwitz partial quotients
of
z
or simply the
partial quotients
of
z
. Let
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be the possibly finite sequences of Gaussian integers given by

\displaystyle p_{0}=0,\quad p_{1}

\displaystyle=1,\quad p_{n+1}=a_{n+1}p_{n}+p_{n-1}\text{ for }n\geq 1,

\displaystyle q_{0}=1,\quad q_{1}

\displaystyle=a_{1},\quad q_{n+1}=a_{n+1}q_{n}+q_{n-1}\text{ for }n\geq 1.

(see
[
41
, Section 2]
). Following
[
14
]
, we refer to
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0},(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
as the
\mathcal{Q}
-pair
of
z
.

Hcfs produce an actual continued fraction representation of complex numbers. This is made precise in Proposition
4.1
. For the rest of the paper, we write

\mathscr{D}:=\mathbb{Z}[i]\setminus\{0,1,i,-1,-i\}.

Sometimes, we refer to an element of
\mathscr{D}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, as a word of length
n
over the alphabet
\mathscr{D}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Take any
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be the possibly finite sequence of partial quotients of
z
and
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
its
\mathcal{Q}
-pair.

i.

[
31
, p. 73]
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
a_{n}
exists, then
a_{n}\in\mathscr{D}
.

ii.

[
41
, Theorem 1]
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
a_{n}
exists, then

\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}=[a_{0};a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]:=a_{0}+\cfrac{1}{a_{1}+\cfrac{1}{\ddots+\cfrac{1}{a_{n}}}}.

iii.

[
14
, Theorem 6.1]
The sequence
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is infinite if and only if
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i)
; in this case,

z=\lim_{n\to\infty}[a_{0};a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}].

For readability and brevity, many of our arguments rely on pictures. Let us discuss Proposition
4.1
.
i
as an example. Figure
1
depicts the set
\iota[\mathfrak{F}]
. An explicit description of
\iota[\mathfrak{F}]
can certainly be obtained using (
3
). However, it is immediate from Figure
1
that, if
a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]
, then

\tau_{a}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\iota[\mathfrak{F}]\neq\varnothing\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad a\in\mathscr{D}.

As a consequence, for any non-zero
z\in\mathfrak{F}
we have
a_{1}(z)=[z^{-1}]\in\mathscr{D}
(see Remark
3.1
) and, therefore,
a_{n}(z)\in\mathscr{D}
whenever it exists.

Figure 1.
The set
\iota[\mathfrak{F}]
.

Proposition
4.2
collects some properties of Hcfs. Although these properties resemble well-established facts on regular continued fractions–except for part
iii
–the known proofs are much more involved.

Proposition 4.2
.

Consider an arbitrary
z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in\mathfrak{F}\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i)
with
\mathcal{Q}
-pair
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
. The next assertions hold:

i.

[
36
, p. 195]
The sequence
(|q_{n}|)_{n\geq 0}
is strictly increasing.

ii.

[
14
, Corollary 5.3]
If
\psi\colon=\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)^{1/2}
, then

|q_{n}|\geq\psi^{n-1}\;\text{ for all }\;n\in\mathbb{N}.

iii.

[
14
, Proposition 3.3]
We have

z=\frac{(a_{n+1}+[a_{n+2},a_{n+3},\ldots])p_{n}+p_{n-1}}{(a_{n+1}+[a_{n+2},a_{n+3},\ldots])q_{n}+q_{n-1}}\;\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

iv.

[
43
, Theorem 1]
We have

\left|z-\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}\right|<\frac{1}{|q_{n}|^{2}}\;\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

We have restricted Proposition
4.2
to numbers in
\mathfrak{F}\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i)
, but it can be easily adapted to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
(including finite sequences of partial quotients).

We may express the correspondence rule of the functions
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
in terms of the sequences
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
41
, Theorem 5]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
be arbitrary. For every
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have

Q_{n}(z;\mathbf{a})=\frac{zp_{n-1}+p_{n}}{zq_{n-1}+q_{n}}=\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}+\frac{(-1)^{n}z}{q_{n}^{2}\left(1+\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}z\right)}.

Moreover, if
z\in\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
(see Definition
5.1
below), then

Q_{n}(z;\mathbf{a})=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n-1},a_{n}+z].

5.
Cylinders and prototype sets

In this section, we introduce the building blocks of the metrical theory of Hcfs: cylinders and prototype sets. After their definitions, we classify them into six categories and provide examples justifying the need for this classification. Later, we study the symmetries of the family of one of these categories: the family of regular cylinders.

5.1.
Definitions and basic properties

In the definition below, the term prototype set is borrowed from
[
29
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
, the
cylinder
of level
n
based on
\mathbf{a}
is the set

\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:a_{1}(z)=a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}(z)=a_{n}\}.

The
prototype set

\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
is
\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=T^{n}[\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})]
.

Figure 2.
Cylinders of level
1
.

Figure
2
depicts cylinders of level
1
.
The next proposition gathers some properties of cylinders and prototype sets stemming directly from their definitions.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
be arbitrary.

i.

The restriction of
T_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
to
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
is a homeomorphism from
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
onto
\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
. Similarly, the restriction of
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
to
\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
is a homeomorphism from
\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
onto
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
.

ii.

We have
\mathcal{C}_{1}(a_{1})=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\mathfrak{F}
.

iii.

If
a_{n+1}\in\mathscr{D}
, then

\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1})=\mathcal{C}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathcal{C}_{n}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n+1})].

In general, for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})=\mathcal{C}_{j}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{j})\cap Q_{j}(\mathcal{C}_{n+1-j}(a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{n+1});(a_{1},\ldots,a_{j})).

The assertion remains true if we replace
\mathcal{C}
with
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
(resp. with
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
).

5.2.
Taxonomy

We introduce a taxonomy of cylinders, prototype sets, sequences, and numbers in
\mathfrak{F}
. To motivate it, we discuss a particular examples of prototype sets using the notation in items
5
and
21
of Section
2
.
The definition of prototype sets yields

\tau_{-2}[\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2)]=\iota[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\tau_{-2}[\mathfrak{F}].

Using (
4
), we can verify
\iota[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\tau_{-2}[\mathfrak{F}]=\tau_{-2}[\mathfrak{F}\setminus\mathbb{D}(1;1)]
, then,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2)=\mathfrak{F}\setminus\mathbb{D}(1;1)
.
Applying equations (
3
) and (
4
), we obtain the set

\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2)]=\iota[\mathfrak{F}]\setminus\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\real(z)>\frac{1}{2}\right\},

which is depicted in Figure
3
. Define

\alpha\colon=\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{2}\;\text{ and }\;\zeta_{1}\colon=-\frac{1}{2}+i\alpha.

Figure 3.
The set
\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2)]
.

Then, for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|m|\geq 2
, we have

\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2)]\cap\tau_{1+im}[\mathfrak{F}]=\begin{cases}\left[\frac{-1-i}{2}+1+im,\frac{-1+i}{2}+1+im\right),&\text{ if }|m|\geq 3,\\[8.61108pt]
\left[\,\overline{\zeta_{1}}+1+2i,\frac{-1+i}{2}+1+2i\right),&\text{ if }m=2,\\[8.61108pt]
\left[\frac{-1-i}{2}+1+2i,\zeta_{1}+1-2i\right),&\text{ if }m=-2,\end{cases}

which implies that

\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2,1+im)=\begin{cases}\left[\frac{-1-i}{2},\frac{-1+i}{2}\right),&\text{ if }|m|\geq 3,\\[8.61108pt]
\left[\,\overline{\zeta_{1}},\frac{-1+i}{2}\right],&\text{ if }m=2,\\[8.61108pt]
\left[\frac{-1-i}{2},\zeta_{1}\right),&\text{ if }m=-2.\end{cases}

Note that
\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2,1+2i)]
is the arch of the circle
C(-1;1)
going from
-2+i+\alpha-\frac{1}{2}
(included) to
-1-i
(excluded), so

\tau_{-2+i}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{2}(-2,-1+2i)]=\left\{\tau_{-2+i}\left(\alpha-\frac{i}{2}\right)\right\};

hence,

\mathfrak{F}_{2}(-2,-1+2i,-2+i)=\left\{\alpha-\frac{i}{2}\right\}.

To sum up, we have shown the next points:

i.

\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(-2)\neq\varnothing
;

ii.

If
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
|m|\geq 2
, then
\mathcal{C}_{2}(-2,1+im)\neq\varnothing
but
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{2}(-2,1+im)=\varnothing
;

iii.

\#\mathcal{C}_{3}(-2,-1+2i,-2+i)=1
.

Remark 5.3
.

From the previous discussion, we also conclude that if
z\in\mathfrak{F}\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i)
satisfies
a_{1}(z)=-2
, then
\real(a_{2}(z))\leq 1
. Similar observations hold for other small digits; for example,
a_{1}(z)=1+i
implies
\imag(z)\leq 0\leq\real(z)
.

In what follows, we denote

\overline{\mathfrak{F}}:=\Cl(\mathfrak{F})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}:=\inte(\mathfrak{F}),

and, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)
, we write

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\Cl(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})),

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\inte(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\Cl(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})),

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\inte(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})).

Definition 5.4
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
is

i.

valid
if
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\neq\varnothing
;

ii.

invalid
if
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\varnothing
;

iii.

regular
if
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\neq\varnothing
;

iv.

irregular
if
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\neq\varnothing
and
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\varnothing
;

v.

extremely irregular
if
\#\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=1
;

vi.

full
if
\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{F}
;

vii.

almost full
if for every
b\in\mathscr{D}
the word
\mathbf{a}b
is regular.

We denote by
\Omega(n)
,
\mathsf{R}(n)
,
\mathsf{Ir}(n)
,
\mathsf{EI}(n)
,
\mathsf{F}(n)
, and
\mathsf{AF}(n)
to be the sets of valid, regular, irregular, extremely irregular, full, almost full words of length
n
respectively.

We extend these classifications to infinite sequences. We say that
\mathbf{a}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathbb{N}}
is

i.

valid
if it is the Hcf of some
z\in\mathfrak{F}
;

ii.

invalid
if it is not valid;

iii.

regular
if it is valid and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

iv.

irregular
if it is valid and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{Ir}(n)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

v.

extremely irregular
if it is valid and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{EI}(n)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

vi.

full
if it is valid and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{F}(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

vii.

almost full
if it is valid and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{AF}(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We denote by
\Omega
,
\mathsf{R}
,
\mathsf{EI}
,
\mathsf{F}
, and
\mathsf{AF}
the set of valid, regular, extremely irregular, full, and almost full words, respectively. We extend in an obvious way the notions of being full, almost full, regular, irregular, and extremely irregular to prototype sets.

Remark 5.5
.

This notation allows us to give an alternative description of prototype sets. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
, we have

\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i)=\{z\in\mathfrak{F}\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i):(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n},a_{1}(z),a_{2}(z),\ldots)\in\Omega\}.

The next proposition is a direct consequence of the definitions of cylinders and full words.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathscr{D}^{n}
be arbitrary, then

\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{F}(n)\;\text{ if and only if }\;\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{n}}[\mathfrak{F}].

A complex number
z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in\mathfrak{F}
is
full
(resp.
almost full
,
regular
,
irregular
,
extremely irregular
) if
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is full (resp. almost full, regular, irregular, extremely irregular). The number
\zeta_{1}
discussed above is an example of an extremely irregular number. It is well established that Lebesgue almost all
z\in\mathfrak{F}
are regular. This follows from the existence of a
T
-ergodic Borel measure equivalent to the Lebesgue measure (see, for example,
[
17
, Proposition 1]
) and Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem.

Definition 5.7
.

An
open cylinder
(resp.
open prototype set
,
closed cylinder
,
closed prototype set
) is a set of the form
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
(resp.
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
,
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
) for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
.

Remark 5.8
.

There are only 13 closed prototype sets (see, for example,
[
17
, Section 2]
). In fact, any closed prototype set is one of the sets depicted in Figure
4
or one of their right-angled rotations. Note that the boundaries of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
,
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2)
, and
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)
consist of four smooth curves whereas the boundary of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2+i)
consists of five smooth curves. We refer to each of these curves as a
side
of the prototype set. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, a
side
of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
is the image under
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
of a side of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
.
Analogous observations hold for open prototype sets.

Figure 4
.
Closed prototype sets

There are sequences
\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\Omega
for which every prefix is valid. This is the case, for example, of the purely periodic sequence

\mathbf{a}=(-2,2i,2,-2i,-2,2i,2,-2i,\ldots).

However, every sequence (finite or infinite) with sufficiently large terms is full. To be more precise, define

\mathcal{E}:=\left\{a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]:|a|\geq\sqrt{8}\right\}.

The following proposition was mentioned (without proof) in
[
24
]
. We provide its proof for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition 5.9
.

We have
\mathcal{E}^{n}\subseteq\mathsf{F}(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathsf{F}
.

Proof.

First, we show inductively the assertion for finite words. Take any
a_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
. We obtain
\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathfrak{F}]\subseteq\iota[\mathfrak{F}]
from Figure
1
. Proposition
5.2
.
ii
then gives
\mathcal{C}_{1}(a_{1})=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathfrak{F}]
and, by Proposition
5.6
,
a_{1}\in\mathsf{F}(1)
. Assume
\mathcal{E}^{N}\subseteq\mathsf{F}(N)
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
and take
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N},a_{N+1})\in\mathcal{E}^{N+1}
. In view of Proposition
5.2
.
iii
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{N+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N+1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathcal{C}_{N}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{N+1})]

\displaystyle=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathfrak{F}\cap\mathcal{C}_{N}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{N+1})]

\displaystyle(\text{by the case }N=1)

\displaystyle=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\mathcal{C}_{N}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{N+1})]

\displaystyle=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\iota\tau_{a_{2}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{N+1}}[\mathfrak{F}].

The last equality follows from the induction hypothesis and Proposition
5.6
. Lastly, by Proposition
5.6
again, we conclude that
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{F}(N+1)
and
\mathcal{E}^{N+1}\subseteq\mathsf{F}(N+1)
follows.

Now we turn to the second assertion. Consider any
\mathbf{a}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathbb{N}}
. By the previous part, we have
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\neq\varnothing
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\epsilon
denote the empty word and define
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\epsilon)=\mathfrak{F}
. We claim that

\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\;\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

The case
n=0
follows from Figure
1
while for an arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{n}}\iota\tau_{a_{n+1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]

\displaystyle=\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{n}}[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{n+1})]

\displaystyle\subseteq\iota\tau_{a_{1}}\cdots\iota\tau_{a_{n}}[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

thus proving the claim.
Parts
ii
and
iv
of Proposition
4.2
imply
|\mathcal{C}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, there is a single element
z
in

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{C}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

which clearly satisfies
z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]
and, thus,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{F}
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

If
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, then
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\Cl\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\Cl\left(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)
.

Proof.

The result is obvious for
n=1
(see Figure
1
). For the general case, use induction on
n
.
∎

For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, write
\Pm(z)\colon=\min\{|\real(z)|,|\imag(z)|\}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
n
be a natural number and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
.

i.

If
\Pm(a_{n})\geq 3
, then
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{F}(n)
.

ii.

If
\|a_{n}\|\geq 3
, then
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{AF}(n)
.

iii.

There exists
d\in\mathscr{D}
such that
\Pm(d)=\|d\|=3
and
\mathbf{a}d\in\mathsf{F}(n+1)
.

Proof.

Parts
i
and
ii
are shown by checking case by case all the open prototype sets. Part
iii
is essentially
[
29
, Lemma 2.6(b)]
.
∎

5.3.
Symmetries of regular cylinders

The symmetries of the Hcfs process are at the core of the geometric properties that
we study. For instance, we extend the symmetries exhibited by open cylinders of level 1 in Figure
2
to open cylinders of arbitrary level. With this in mind, consider the maps
\mathsf{rot},\mathsf{mir}_{1}:\overline{\mathbb{C}}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}}
given by

\forall z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\quad\mathsf{rot}(z)\colon=iz\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{mir}_{1}(z)\colon=\overline{z}

and call
\mathsf{Di}_{8}
the group of isometries generated by
\mathsf{rot}
and
\mathsf{mir}_{1}
along with the composition of functions. Clearly,
\mathsf{Di}_{8}
is the dihedral group of order
8
. In what follows, the juxtaposition of elements of
\mathsf{Di}_{8}
,
\iota
, and the maps
\tau_{a}
stands for their composition; for example,
\mathsf{rot}\,\mathsf{mir}_{1}(z)=i\overline{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

We will denote the cyclic group generated by
\mathsf{rot}
by
\mathsf{Rot}
. If
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, we write
A\equiv B\mod\mathsf{Rot}
whenever there is some
f\in\mathsf{Rot}
such that
f[A]=B
. We likewise define
A\equiv B\mod\mathsf{Di}_{8}
.

For reference, we gather some straightforward observations in the next proposition.

Proposition 5.12
.

The following assertions hold:

i.

Every
f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
gives a bijection from
\mathscr{D}
onto itself and
\|f(a)\|=\|a\|
for all
a\in\mathscr{D}
.

ii.

\mathsf{rot}\,\iota=\iota\,\mathsf{rot}^{-1}
,
\mathsf{rot}^{-1}\,\iota=\iota\,\mathsf{rot}
.

iii.

For all
a\in\mathscr{D}
we have
\mathsf{rot}\,\tau_{a}=\tau_{\mathsf{rot}(a)}\,\mathsf{rot}
,
\mathsf{rot}^{-1}\tau_{a}=\tau_{\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a)}\,\mathsf{rot}^{-1}
.

iv.

For all
a\in\mathscr{D}
we have
\mathsf{rot}\,\iota\,\tau_{a}=\iota\,\tau_{\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a)}\mathsf{rot}^{-1}
and
\mathsf{rot}^{-1}\,\iota\,\tau_{a}=\iota\,\tau_{\mathsf{rot}(a)}\,\mathsf{rot}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
, there are some
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
such that

f\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right]=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\quad\text{ and }\quad b_{j}=f_{j}(a_{j})\;\text{ for all }\;j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

We can replace
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}
with
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
in the previous equality.

Proof.

It is enough to show the result when
f
is one of the generators
\mathsf{rot}
or
\mathsf{mir}_{1}
. Assume that
f=\mathsf{rot}
and let us demonstrate inductively that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
we have

(5)

\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right]=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}),\mathsf{rot}(a_{2}),\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{3}),\ldots,\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{n}}(a_{n})\right).

Take any
a_{1}\in\mathscr{D}
. First, we show that

(6)

\mathsf{rot}[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{1})]=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})\right)\;\text{ and }\;\mathsf{rot}^{-1}\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}\left(a_{1}\right)\right]=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}\left(\mathsf{rot}(a_{1})\right).

To this end, note that a complex number
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{1})
if and only if there is some
w\in\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{1})
such that

z=\frac{1}{a_{1}+w}.

This is equivalent to

\mathsf{rot}(z)=\frac{1}{\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})+\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(w)},

or, equivalently,
\iota\,\mathsf{rot}(z)=\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})+\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(w)
, and since
\mathsf{rot}(z)\in\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}
,
\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})\in\mathscr{D}
, and
\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(w)\in\mathfrak{F}
, we have

a_{1}\left(\mathsf{rot}(z)\right)=\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}).

We may, thus, conclude

\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{1})\right]\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})\right).

Moreover, because
\mathsf{rot}
maps open sets onto open sets, we even have

\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{1})\right]\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})\right).

In a similar fashion, we may show
\mathsf{rot}^{-1}[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})\right)]\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{1})
and we get the first identity in (
6
). The second equality follows from the first one.

Assume (
5
) is true for some
n=N\in\mathbb{N}
. Take any
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N},a_{N+1})\in\mathsf{R}(N+1)
and let us show that

(7)

\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{N+1}(\mathbf{a})\right]=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{N+1}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}),\mathsf{rot}(a_{2}),\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{3}),\ldots,\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{N+1}}(a_{N+1})\right).

Proposition
5.2
.
iii
implies that

(8)

\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}_{N+1}^{\circ}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N+1})\right]=\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}_{N}^{\circ}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})\right]\cap\mathsf{rot}\left[Q_{N}(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{N+1});(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})\right].

We apply the induction hypothesis on the first term to the right in order to get

(9)

\mathsf{rot}\left[\mathcal{C}_{N}^{\circ}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})\right]=\mathcal{C}_{N}^{\circ}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{N}}(a_{N})\right).

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
8
), observe that if
N=2
then

\mathsf{rot}\,\iota\,\tau_{a_{1}}\,\iota\,\tau_{a_{2}}=(\iota\tau_{\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})}\,\mathsf{rot}^{-1})\iota\,\tau_{a_{2}}=\iota\,\tau_{\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1})}\,\iota\,\tau_{\mathsf{rot}(a_{2})}\,\mathsf{rot}

and
\mathsf{rot}\circ Q(\,\cdot\,;(a_{1},a_{2}))=Q(\,\cdot\,;(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}),\mathsf{rot}(a_{2}))\circ\mathsf{rot}
. In general, from Proposition
5.12
.
iv
, we obtain

\mathsf{rot}\circ Q(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})=Q(\,\cdot\,;(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}),\mathsf{rot}(a_{2}),\ldots,\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{N}}(a_{2}))\circ\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{N}}.

Thus, depending on the parity of
N
, we use one of the identities in (
6
) to conclude

Q_{N}(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(a_{N+1});(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}))=Q_{N}\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\circ}\left(\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{N+1}}(a_{N+1})\right);\left(\mathsf{rot}^{-1}(a_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{rot}^{(-1)^{N}}(a_{N})\right)\right).

We now apply Proposition
5.12
.
iv
to obtain (
7
).

Using a similar yet simpler inductive argument, we may show that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, we have

(10)

\mathsf{mir}_{1}\left[\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right]=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}\left(\mathsf{mir}_{1}(a_{1}),\mathsf{mir}_{1}(a_{2}),\ldots,\mathsf{mir}_{1}(a_{n})\right).

The assertion for closed cylinders follows from the proof above, Proposition
5.10
, and the fact that
\mathsf{mir}_{1}
and
\mathsf{rot}
are homeomorphisms from
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
onto itself.
∎

Define
\mathsf{mir}_{2}\colon=\mathsf{rot}^{2}\,\mathsf{mir}_{1}
, so
\mathsf{mir}_{2}(z)=-\overline{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
. When
f=\mathsf{mir}_{2}
, we can use equations (
10
) and (
5
) to determine the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
appearing in Proposition
5.13
.

Corollary 5.14
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, we have

(11)

\mathsf{mir}_{2}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)=\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}\left(\mathsf{mir}_{2}(a_{1}),\mathsf{mir}_{2}(a_{2}),\ldots,\mathsf{mir}_{2}(a_{n})\right).

5.4.
Size of regular cylinders

In this subsection, we recall some estimates of the diameter and the Lebesgue measure
\mathfrak{m}
of regular cylinders in terms of the sequence
(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
. See item
23
in Section
2
for the definition of
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)
, and
\mathbf{b}\in\Omega(m)
be arbitrary.

i.

[
29
, Lemma 2.7]

There is an absolute constant
0<\kappa_{1}<1
such that if
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, then

\frac{\kappa_{1}}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}\leq|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{2}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}

and

\frac{\pi\kappa_{1}}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{4}}\leq\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))\leq\frac{\pi}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{4}}.

ii.

If
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in\Omega(m+n)
, then

\frac{1}{5\sqrt{2}}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|\|q_{m}(\mathbf{b})\|\leq\|q_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})\|\leq 6\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|\|q_{m}(\mathbf{b})\|.

iii.

There is an absolute constant
\kappa_{2}>1
such that if
\mathbf{a}
,
\mathbf{b}
, and
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}
are regular, then

\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})||\mathcal{C}_{m}(\mathbf{b})|\leq|\mathcal{C}_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})|\leq\kappa_{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})||\mathcal{C}_{m}(\mathbf{b})|.

iv.

There is an absolute constant
\kappa_{3}>1
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)
, and
A\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
we have

\frac{1}{\kappa_{3}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right||A|<\left|Q_{n}(A;\mathbf{a})\right|<\kappa_{3}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right||A|.

Proof.

For part
ii
, we know that
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in\Omega(n+m)
implies

(12)

\frac{1}{5}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})||q_{m}(\mathbf{b})|\leq|q_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})|\leq 3|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})||q_{m}(\mathbf{b})|

(for a proof, see
[
29
, Lemma 2.2.(g)]
). Since
\|z\|\leq|z|\leq\sqrt{2}\|z\|
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\|q_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})\|\leq|q_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})|\leq 3|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})||q_{m}(\mathbf{b})|\leq 6\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|\|q_{m}(\mathbf{b})\|

and, similarly,

\|q_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})\|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|q_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})|\geq\frac{1}{5\sqrt{2}}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|\|q_{m}(\mathbf{b})\|.

For part
iii
, combine parts
i
and
ii
.

Part
iv
is a consequence of Proposition
4.3
and the next inequality, which follows from Proposition
4.2
.
i
and
z\in\mathfrak{F}
,

(13)

1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\leq\left|1+\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}z\right|\leq 1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}.

A similar argument is developed in full detail in the proof of Proposition
6.4
.
∎

6.
Geometric constructions

In this section, we count how many sets of a particular kind are intersected by a disc of small radius and center in
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
. To this end, we consider two possible geometric configurations, which we call
constructions
. The results in this section will be used for the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.1
.

We start off by proving that the corners of
\mathfrak{F}
cannot be attained using closed cylinders of level at least
2
(Proposition
6.2
). Let
\mathsf{Esq}
be the set of corners
1
1

1

The notation
\mathsf{Esq}
comes from the word
esquina
, which is Spanish for
corner
.
of
\mathfrak{F}
; that is,

\mathsf{Esq}\colon=\left\{\frac{1+i}{2},\frac{-1+i}{2},\frac{-1-i}{2},\frac{1-i}{2}\right\}.

Note that

(14)

\iota[\mathsf{Esq}]\colon=\left\{1-i,-1-i,-1+i,1+i\right\}\subseteq\mathscr{D}.

We will write

\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})\colon=\{f(\zeta_{1}):f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}\}\;\text{ and }\;\mathsf{Rot}(\zeta_{1})\colon=\{f(\zeta_{1}):f\in\mathsf{Rot}\}.

Proposition 6.1
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, we have

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in\mathscr{D}\\
\mathbf{a}d\in\mathsf{R}(n+1)\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}d)=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\setminus\{[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]\}.

Proof.

From Figure
2
, we immediately conclude that

(15)

\bigcup_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d)=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{0\}.

We, however, provide a full proof to emphasize the clarity we gain by relying on pictures. First, for each
d\in\mathscr{D}
and
z\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(d)
, there is some
w\in\mathfrak{F}
such that
z=\frac{1}{d+w}
, so

|z|\geq\frac{1}{|d|+|w|}\geq\frac{1}{|d|+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}},\;\text{ and }\;0\not\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d).

This shows the inclusion

\bigcup_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d)\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{0\}.

For the other inclusion, take any
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{0\}
. If
z\in\mathfrak{F}
, then
z\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(a_{1}(z))\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{1}(z))
. If
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\mathfrak{F}
, then
z
belongs to either the upper or the right side of
\mathfrak{F}
. Hence, there is some
\mathsf{mir}\in\{\mathsf{mir}_{1},\mathsf{mir}_{2}\}
such that
\mathsf{mir}(z)\in\mathfrak{F}
, so

\mathsf{mir}(z)\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{1}(\mathsf{mir}(z)).

Then, in view of (
10
) or (
11
), we have

z\in\mathsf{mir}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{1}(\mathsf{mir}(z))=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(\mathsf{mir}(a_{1}(\mathsf{mir}(z))).

For the general case, take any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
. Equation (
15
) implies that

\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\bigcup_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d)=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\setminus\{0\}.

Lastly, apply
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
on both sides of the previous equality and conclude.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, we have

\mathsf{Esq}\cap\bigcup_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\varnothing.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
be arbitrary. By Proposition
5.2
.
iii
,

\displaystyle\iota[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})]

\displaystyle=\iota\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\iota\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})]\right]

\displaystyle=\iota[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{1})]\cap\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})]

\displaystyle=\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(a_{1})]\cap\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})]

\displaystyle=\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})].

Form (
14
), we obtain
\iota[\mathsf{Esq}]\cap\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\subseteq\{a_{1}\}
, so

\displaystyle\iota[\mathsf{Esq}\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})]

\displaystyle=\iota[\mathsf{Esq}]\cap\iota[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})]

\displaystyle\subseteq\iota[\mathsf{Esq}]\cap\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})]

\displaystyle\subseteq\iota[\mathsf{Esq}]\cap\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})]

\displaystyle\subseteq\{a_{1}\}\cap\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})]

\displaystyle=\tau_{a_{1}}\left[\{0\}\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(a_{1})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq\tau_{a_{1}}\left[\{0\}\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})\right]

\displaystyle=\varnothing.

(by Proposition
15
)

∎

6.1.
First geometric construction

The main result of this subsection is Theorem
6.3
. It tells us that any disc of sufficiently small radius and center in a closed cylinder of level
n
intersects at most six regular cylinders of level
n
. More precisely, given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, the set of
neighbors
of
\mathbf{a}
is
2
2

2

The notation
\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
comes from the Spanish word
vecino
, which means
neighbor
.

\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\left\{\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n):\mathbf{b}\neq\mathbf{a}\text{ and }\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\neq\varnothing\right\}.

For
\rho>0
and
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
, define

G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho)\colon=\{\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n):\mathbb{D}(z;\rho|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\neq\varnothing\}.

Theorem 6.3
.

There is a constant
\rho>0
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
and
z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
we have

\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho)\leq 6,\quad G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho)\subseteq\{\mathbf{a}\}\cup\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a}),

and

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho)}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

We start with an auxiliary result.
It is well known that Möbius transformations (or fractional linear transformations) map discs in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
onto discs in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover, if
t
is a Möbius transformation and
D=\mathbb{D}(z;r)
for some
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
r>0
, then
t[D]
is also a disc in
\mathbb{C}
whenever the pole of
t
is not contained in
D
. In this case, the center of
t[D]
might not be
t(z)
, but there is some
\rho>0
such that

\mathbb{D}\left(t(z);\rho\right)\subseteq t\left[\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right].

Proposition
6.4
below provides some values for
\rho
when
t=Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
for some
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that, by the triangle inequality, for all
w_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\rho>0
, and
w_{2}\in\mathbb{D}(w_{1};\rho)
we have

(16)

\mathbb{D}\left(w_{2};\rho-|w_{1}-w_{2}|\right)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\left(w_{1};\rho\right).

Furthermore, the next equality holds:

\rho-|w_{1}-w_{2}|=\inf_{0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi}\left|w_{2}-(w_{1}+\rho e^{i\theta})\right|.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
be arbitrary. If
w\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
and
0<r<1
are such that
\mathbb{D}(w;r)\subseteq\mathbb{D}(0;1)
, then
Q_{n}(\mathbb{D}(w;r);\mathbf{a})
is a disc in the complex plane and

\mathbb{D}\left(Q_{n}(w;\mathbf{a});\frac{r}{2\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\subseteq Q_{n}(\mathbb{D}(w;r);\mathbf{a}).

Proof.

The set
Q_{n}(\mathbb{D}(w;r);\mathbf{a})
is a disc on the Riemann sphere.
Writing
q_{n}=q_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and
q_{n-1}=q_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
, Proposition
4.2
.
i
and Proposition
4.3
tell us that
\overline{\mathbb{D}}(w;r)
does not contain the pole of
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
. Then, there are some
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\rho>0
such that

Q_{n}(\mathbb{D}(w;r);\mathbf{a})=\mathbb{D}(w^{\prime};\rho).

Next, we show the inclusion in the statement. For any
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|Q_{n}(w;\mathbf{a})-Q_{n}(w+re^{i\theta};\mathbf{a})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{w}{q_{n}^{2}\left(1+\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}w\right)}-\frac{w+re^{i\theta}}{q_{n}^{2}\left(1+\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}(w+re^{i\theta})\right)}\right|\quad\text{(by Proposition \ref{Prop:QnCorrespRule})}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{|q_{n}|^{2}\left|1+\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}w\right|\left|1+\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}(w+re^{i\theta})\right|}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r}{2\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|.\qquad\text{(by Proposition \ref{Prop:SizeEstimates}.\ref{Prop:SizeEstimates:i} and }w\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}})

As a consequence, since
Q_{n}(C(w;r);\mathbf{a})=C(w^{\prime};\rho)
, we have

\inf_{0\leq\theta<2\pi}\left|Q_{n}(w;\mathbf{a})-(w^{\prime}+\rho e^{i\theta})\right|\geq\frac{r}{2\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|.

Lastly, in view of
Q_{n}(w;\mathbf{a})\in\mathbb{D}(w^{\prime};\rho)
, we appeal to (
16
) to conclude the result.
∎

6.1.1.
Proof of Theorem
6.3

We split the proof of Theorem
6.3
into five parts (lemmas
6.5
through
6.9
) depending on the position of
z
with respect to the boundary of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
. We denote the boundary of a given
A\subseteq\mathbb{C}
by
\Fr(A)
. The constants
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{5}
in the lemmas below are absolute.

Lemma 6.5
.

There is a constant
\rho_{1}>0
such that for all
f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
for which
\xi:=f(\zeta_{1})\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\Fr(\mathfrak{F})
, we have

G_{n}(\xi,\mathbf{a},\rho_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\{\mathbf{a}\},\quad\#G_{n}(\xi,\mathbf{a},\rho_{1})\leq 3,

and

\mathbb{D}(\xi;\rho_{1}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in G_{n}(\xi,\mathbf{a},\rho_{1})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

Lemma 6.6
.

Take any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\Fr(\mathfrak{F})\neq\varnothing
, and
z\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F})
such that

i.

There exists
\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
satisfying
\mathbf{a}\neq\mathbf{c}
and
z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{c})
;

ii.

If
z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a)
, then
a=a_{1}
.

There is a constant
\rho_{2}>0
for which

G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{2})\subseteq\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\{\mathbf{a}\},\quad\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{2})\leq 3,

and

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{2})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

Lemma 6.7
.

There is a constant
\rho_{3}>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
with
\Fr(\mathfrak{F})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\neq\varnothing
, and any
z\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, we have

G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{3})\subseteq\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\{\mathbf{a}\},\quad\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{3})\leq 3,

and

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{3}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{3})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

Lemma 6.8
.

There is a constant
\rho_{4}>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
z\in\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}\cap\Fr(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))
, we have

G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{4})\subseteq\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\{\mathbf{a}\},\quad\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{4})\leq 6,

and

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{4}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{4})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

Lemma 6.9
.

There is a constant
\rho_{5}>0
such that if
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
z\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, then

G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{5})\subseteq\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\{\mathbf{a}\},\quad\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{5})\leq 6,

and

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{5}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{5})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

6.1.2.
Proof of Lemma
6.5

Let us assume that
f=\mathsf{mir}_{1}
, so
\xi=\mathsf{mir}_{1}(\zeta_{1})=-\frac{1}{2}-i\alpha
, the same argument will hold for any
f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
by Subsection
5.3
.
Define the sequences
\mathbf{d}^{1}\colon=(d_{1,n})_{n\geq 1}
,
\mathbf{d}^{2}\colon=(d_{2,n})_{n\geq 1}
, and
\mathbf{d}^{3}\colon=(d_{3,n})_{n\geq 1}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}^{1}

\displaystyle\colon=(-2,-2i,2,2i,-2,-2i,2,2i,\ldots),

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}^{2}

\displaystyle\colon=(-2+i,2i,2,-2i,-2,2i,2,-2i,-2,\ldots),

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}^{3}

\displaystyle\colon=(-2+i,1+2i,-2,-2i,2,2i,-2,-2i,2,2i,\ldots);

that is,
\mathbf{d}^{1}
,
(d_{2,n})_{n\geq 2}
, and
(d_{3,n})_{n\geq 3}
are purely periodic sequences of period length
4
.

The proof technique of the next proposition will be used extensively in the forthcoming arguments.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A word
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
satisfies
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
if and only if it is a prefix of
\mathbf{d}^{1}
,
\mathbf{d}^{2}
or
\mathbf{d}^{3}
.

Proof.

Consider
n=1
. By Figure
1
, the only numbers
a_{1}\in\mathscr{D}
satisfying
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(a_{1})
are
a_{1}=-2
and
a_{1}=-2+i
. Alternatively, from

-\frac{5}{2}<\real(\xi^{-1})<-\frac{3}{2}\;\text{ and }\;\imag(\xi^{-1})=\frac{1}{2},

we conclude
\xi^{-1}\in\tau_{a_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]
if and only if
a_{1}\in\{-2,-2+i\}
.

Now consider
n=2
. Let
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathscr{D}^{2}
be such that

(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathsf{R}(2)\;\text{ and }\;\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(a_{1},a_{2}).

By the case
n=1
, we must have
a_{1}\in\{-2,-2+i\}
. First, assume that
a_{1}=-2
. We claim that
a_{2}=-2i
is the single
a_{2}\in\mathscr{D}
satisfying

(-2,a_{2})\in\mathsf{R}(2)\;\text{ and }\;T_{1}(\xi;-2)\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}(-2)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(a_{2}).

Figure 5.
Possible configurations

Indeed, consider the set
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2)=\tau_{2}\iota[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)]\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
, which is the first configuration depicted in Figure
5
. We have distinguished the point
T_{2}(\xi;-2)
in red, the arch
T_{2}(\Fr(\mathfrak{F})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(2);-2)
in blue and the line segment
T_{2}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2+i);-2)
in green. We apply
\iota
on
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2)
and obtain the set
\iota[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2)]
, which is depicted in Figure
6
.

Figure 6.
The set
\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{1}(-2)]
and the disc
\mathbb{D}\left(\iota T_{1}(\xi;-2);\tfrac{1}{10}\right)
.

Observe that

\iota\tau_{2}\iota(\xi)\in\tau_{-2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cap\tau_{1-2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}].

However,
(-2,-2i)\in\mathsf{R}(2)
and
(-2,1-2i)\not\in\mathsf{R}(2)
, so
(a_{1},a_{2})=(-2,-2i)
. This shows our claim. We also conclude that

\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2i)=T_{2}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(2);(-2,-2i))\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}},

thus obtaining the second configuration in Figure
5
.
In the second configuration in Figure
5
, the red point is
T_{2}(\xi;(-2,-2i))
;
the green arch is the restriction to
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
of the image under
T_{2}(\,\cdot\,;(-2,-2i))
of the green segment in the first configuration, and a similar interpretation for the blue segment.

We may repeat the argument to conclude that
a_{3}=2
is the only solution
a_{3}\in\mathscr{D}
of

(-2,-2i,a_{3})\in\mathsf{R}(3)\;\text{ and }\;\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{3}(-2,-2i,a_{3}).

Moreover, we get
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2)=T_{3}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(2);(-2,-2i,2))\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
, and obtain the third configuration in Figure
5
. Likewise, we can show that
a_{4}=2i
is the only
a_{4}\in\mathscr{D}
for which

(-2,-2i,2,a_{4})\in\mathsf{R}(4)\;\text{ and }\;\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{3}(-2,-2i,2,a_{4}).

This argument will lead us to the fourth configuration in Figure
5
, which depicts the set
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2i)=T_{4}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(2);(-2,-2i,2,2i))
. Finally, the same strategy shows that
a_{5}=-2
is the only solution of

(-2,-2i,2,2i,a_{5})\in\mathsf{R}(5)\;\text{ and }\;\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{5}(-2,-2i,2,2i,a_{5}).

and we obtain again the first configuration in Figure
5
.
Therefore, we conclude that if
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
satisfy
a_{1}=-2
and
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, then
\mathbf{a}=\prefi(\mathbf{d}^{1};n)
.

The proof is similar when
a_{1}=-2+i
. First, we show that
a_{2}\in\{2i,1+2i\}
. Consider the set
\iota[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2+i)]\cap(\tau_{2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cup\tau_{1+2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}])
, depicted in Figure
7
. We have distinguished the point
T(\xi;-2+i)
in red, the arch
T(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2+i);-2+i)
in green, and the arch
T(\Fr(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2+i);-2+i)
in blue. We conclude
a_{2}\in\{2i,1+2i\}
arguing as above. Furthermore, we may proceed as in the case
a_{1}=-2
to show that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
and
(a_{1},a_{2})=(-2+i,2i)
(resp.
(a_{1},a_{2})=(-2+i,1+2i)
), we have
\mathbf{a}=\prefi(\mathbf{d}^{2};n)
(resp.
\mathbf{a}=\prefi(\mathbf{d}^{3};n)
).
∎

Figure 7.
The set
\iota[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2+i)]\cap(\tau_{2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cup\tau_{1+2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}])
and the disc
\mathbb{D}\left(\iota T_{1}(\xi;-2+i);\tfrac{1}{10}\right)
.

Corollary 6.11
.

For some absolute constants, we have that if
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
are such that
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})
, then
|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\asymp|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{b})|
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The corollary is obvious if
n\in\{1,2\}
, for there are finitely many cylinders satisfying the hypothesis. Suppose that
n\geq 3
. Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
,
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
be such that
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})
. By Proposition
6.10
, there is some
f
in the group generated by
\mathsf{mir}_{1}
and
\mathsf{mir}_{2}
such that
(a_{3},\ldots,a_{n})=\left(f(b_{3}),\ldots,f(b_{n})\right)
. Then, Proposition
5.13
and Corollary
5.14
give

\left|\mathcal{C}_{n-2}(a_{3},\ldots,a_{n})\right|=|\mathcal{C}_{n-2}(b_{3},\ldots,b_{n})|.

Moreover, we also have

\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=Q_{2}\left(\mathcal{C}_{n-2}(a_{3},\ldots,a_{n});(a_{1},a_{2})\right).

Since there are only three options for
(a_{1},a_{2})
, for some absolute constants we have

\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|\asymp\left|\mathcal{C}_{n-2}(a_{3},\ldots,a_{n})\right|.

Therefore, we conclude

\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|\asymp\left|\mathcal{C}_{n-2}(a_{3},\ldots,a_{n})\right|=\left|\mathcal{C}_{n-2}(b_{3},\ldots,b_{n})\right|\asymp\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\right|.

∎

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

The lemma will be proven once we show the existence of some
\rho_{1}>0
such that for any
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(17)

\mathbb{D}\left(\xi;\rho_{1}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{d}^{j})\right|\right)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\bigcup_{k=1}^{3}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\left(d_{k,1},\ldots,d_{k,n}\right).

First, note that every small
r^{\prime}>0
verifies

(18)

\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\cap\mathbb{D}(\xi;r^{\prime})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2+i).

Indeed, by Figure
8
, for
0<r<\frac{1}{10}
we have

\mathbb{D}\left(\iota(\xi);r\right)\cap\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}\right]\subseteq\left(\tau_{-2}\left[\mathfrak{F}\right]\cup\tau_{-2+i}\left[\mathfrak{F}\right]\right)\cap\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}\right].

Figure 8.
The set
\iota[\mathfrak{F}]\cap(\tau_{-2}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cup\tau_{-2+i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}])
and the disc
\mathbb{D}\left(\iota(\xi),1/10\right)
.

Then, we apply
\iota
to obtain (
18
) for every small
r^{\prime}>0
. This proves (
17
) for
n=1
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Next, we show the existence of some constant
\tilde{\rho}_{1}>0
such that for
n=2
, we have

(19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\left(\xi;\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(-2,-2i)\right|\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(-2,-2i),

(20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\left(\xi;\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(d_{j,1},d_{j,2})\right|\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d_{j,1})

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(-2+i,2i)\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(-2+i,1+2i)\;\text{ for }j=1,2.

As in the proof of Proposition
6.10
, we conclude from Figure
6
that

\mathbb{D}\left(\iota\left(T_{1}(\xi;-2);\frac{1}{10}\right)\right)\cap\iota\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2)\right]\subseteq\tau_{-2i}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cap\iota\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2)\right].

Because of
\iota[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(-2)]=\iota\tau_{2}\,\iota\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)\right]
, we apply
\tau_{2i}
on both sides to get

\mathbb{D}\left(T_{2}(\xi;-2,-2i);\frac{1}{10}\right)\cap T_{2}(\mathcal{C}_{1}(-2);(-2,-2i))\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\cap T_{2}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2);(-2,-2i)).

We now apply
Q_{2}(\,\cdot\,,(-2,-2i))
and obtain

\displaystyle Q_{2}\left(\mathbb{D}\left(T_{2}(\xi;(-2,-2i));\frac{1}{10}\right),(-2,-2i)\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)

\displaystyle\subseteq Q_{2}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}};(-2,-2i))\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-2)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(-2,-2i).

By Proposition
6.4
, there is some
\tilde{\rho}_{1}>0
for which (
19
) is true. Similarly, we can show (
20
) using Figure
7
. Furthermore, essentially the same argument and the proof of Proposition
6.10
allow us to conclude that, for
n\geq 3
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, the same
\tilde{\rho}_{1}>0
satisfies

(21)

\mathbb{D}\left(\xi;\tilde{\rho}_{1}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}\left(d_{j,1},\ldots,d_{j,n}\right)\right|\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}\left(d_{j,1},\ldots,d_{j,n-1}\right)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\left(d_{j,1},\ldots,d_{j,n}\right).

Lastly, in view of Corollary
6.11
, we conclude the existence of some
\rho_{1}>0
such that (
17
) holds for all
n
and
j
.
∎

6.1.3.
Proof of Lemma
6.6

Proposition
6.10
lies at the heart of the proof of Lemma
6.5
. It characterizes the three closed cylinders containing a given
\xi\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})
. We will revisit this argument in the proof of Lemma
6.6
and afterward. We will also use the fact that the only closed cylinders intersecting
\mathsf{Esq}
are
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(\varepsilon)\colon=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(1+i)
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(1-i)
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-1+i)
, and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(-1-i)
(see Proposition
6.2
).

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
b,c\in\mathscr{D}
such that
b\neq c
, and
\mathbf{a}b,\mathbf{a}c\in\mathsf{R}(n+1)
. Assume that there is some
w\in\mathbb{C}
such that

w\in\Fr\left(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(b)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(c).

Then, the next assertions hold:

i.

\tau_{-b}\iota(w)\in\mathsf{Esq}\cup\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})
.

ii.

The numbers
b
and
c
are the only solutions
d\in\mathscr{D}
to

\tau_{-d}\iota(w)\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathbf{a}d\in\mathsf{R}(n+1).

iii.

If
\tau_{-b}\iota(w)\in\mathsf{Esq}
, then the disc
\iota[\mathbb{D}(\iota(w);\frac{1}{10})]
intersects exactly one side of
\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))
.

Proof.

The three parts are shown by checking the possible forms of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
. For clarity, we briefly discuss one option in the first point.
Assume that
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}(-2)
. By Figure
3
, when
|b|\leq\sqrt{5}
and
|c|\leq\sqrt{5}
, any number belonging to
\iota[\Fr\left(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(b)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(c)]
is a Gaussian-integral translate of some element in
\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})
. And if
|b|\geq 3
or
|c|\geq 3
, then any number belonging to
\iota[\Fr\left(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(b)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(c)]
is a Gaussian-integral translate of some point in
\mathsf{Esq}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
be the minimal integer for which there is some
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that

z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{c}),\quad(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}),\quad\text{ and }\quad a_{k+1}\neq c_{k+1}.

Then, we have

w\colon=T_{k}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-k}(a_{k+1},\ldots,a_{n})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-k}(c_{k+1},\ldots,c_{n})\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}).

From
a_{k+1}\neq c_{k+1}
we get
z\in\Fr(\mathcal{C}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))
and, thus,
w\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))
. Therefore,

w\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{k+1})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(c_{k+1})\cap\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))

and, by Proposition
6.12
.
i
, we must have either

\tau_{-a_{k+1}}\iota(w)\in\mathsf{Esq}\;\text{ or }\;\tau_{-a_{k+1}}\iota(w)\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1}).

We discuss both possibilities separately.

Case
\tau_{-a_{k+1}}\iota(w)\in\mathsf{Esq}
.
Proposition
6.12
.
ii
tells us there is a single possibility for
c_{k+1}
. Moreover, in view of

\iota(w)\in\tau_{a_{k+1}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-k-1}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})\cap\mathsf{Esq}\right],

we must have
k\in\{n-1,n-2\}
by Proposition
6.2
.

First, assume that
k=n-1
. Then,
\mathbf{c}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1},c_{n})
and, by Proposition
6.12
.
iii
, the disc
\iota[\mathbb{D}(\iota(w);\frac{1}{10})]
intersects only one side of
\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}))
. From

\mathbb{D}\left(\iota(w);\frac{1}{10}\right)\cap\iota\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\right]\subseteq\left(\tau_{a_{n}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cup\tau_{c_{n}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\right)\cap\iota\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\right]

we get

\iota\left[\mathbb{D}\left(\iota(w);\frac{1}{10}\right)\cap\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\right]\right]\subseteq\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{n})\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(c_{n})\right)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}).

We now apply
Q_{k}(\,\cdot\,;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))=Q_{n-1}(\,\cdot\,;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}))
and use Proposition
6.12
to conclude the existence of some absolute constant
\rho_{2,2}>0
such that

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\frac{\rho_{2,2}|\mathcal{C}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})|}{|a_{n}|^{2}}\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{c}).

Hence, by Proposition
5.15
.
i
, there is some absolute constant
\rho_{2,3}>0
such that

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2,3}|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{c}).

To finalize the proof of this case, we must verify that

(22)

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2,3}|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}=\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2,3}|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}).

To this end, note that the disc
\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2,3}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)
intersects exactly one side of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, which is the one containing
z
. This side is a line segment contained in
\Fr(\mathfrak{F})
. Hence,
\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2,3}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)
only intersects one side of
\mathcal{C}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
. If there was some
\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n-1)
,
\mathbf{b}\neq(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
, such that
\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{2,3}|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)
had an inner point of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-1}(\mathbf{b})
, then this disc would have to intersect at least two sides of
