\mathcal{C}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
. This proves (
22
).

When
n=k-2
, we proceed in a similar fashion, so we omit the proof. However, we note that in this case we must have
|a_{n}|=|c_{n}|=|1+i|=\sqrt{2}
(see Figure
2
).

Case
\tau_{-a_{k+1}}\iota(w)\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta)
.
After checking all the possible options for
\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, we may say that there are
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
such that

\iota(w)\in\{\tau_{a_{k+1}}f_{1}(\zeta_{1})\}\cap\{\tau_{c_{k+1}}f_{2}(\zeta_{1})\}\cap\Fr(\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})]).

Figure
9
depicts all the possible configurations
\mod\mathsf{Rot}
for
\iota(w)
(in red) and
\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})]
(gray). For example, case (c) arises from

z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{3}(-2,4i,1+i)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{3}(-2,4i,2+i)\cap\Fr(\mathfrak{F}).

Figure 9.
Possible location of
\iota(w)
in
\iota[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})]
.

We are now essentially in the situation of Lemma
6.5
. Arguing accordingly, we conclude the existence of some constant
\rho_{2,3}>0
for which

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\left(\iota(w);\rho_{2,3}|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|\right)

\displaystyle\cap\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})]\subseteq

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n-k-2}(c_{k+2},\ldots,c_{n}).

As in the proof of (
22
), we may conclude the existence of some constant
\rho_{2,4}>0
such that

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{2,4}|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},b_{k+1},\ldots,b_{n}).

Lastly, we choose
\rho_{2}\colon=\min\{\rho_{2,2},\rho_{2,4}\}
and the lemma follows. Observe that in case (a) of Figure
9
, we have
\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{2})=2
and
\#G_{n}(z,\mathbf{a},\rho_{2})=3
in cases (b) and (c).
∎

6.1.4.
Proof of Lemma
6.7

Let
n
,
\mathbf{a}
, and
z
be as in the statement. For
\rho_{2}>0
as in Lemma
6.6
and define

\delta\colon=\min\left\{\frac{1}{10},\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}\frac{\rho_{2}}{2}\right\}.

Since
z\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, we have
z\in\Fr(\mathcal{C}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}))
, so

\iota T_{n-1}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}))\in\Fr\left(\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}]\right)

and there are at most two solutions
d\in\mathscr{D}
of

(23)

\tau_{d}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})]\cap\mathbb{D}(\iota(T_{n-1}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}));\delta))\neq\varnothing

one of them being
d=a_{n}
(cf. Lemma
6.12
.
ii
).

When
d=a_{n}
is the only solution of (
23
), we may show as in Lemma
6.5
that

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\frac{\delta}{2\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a}).

Assume that (
23
) has two different solutions, say
d=a_{n}
and
d=b
. Then, we can single out some
\xi\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})\cup\mathsf{Esq}
for which

\mathbb{D}\left(\iota\left(T_{n-1}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\right);\delta))\right)\cap\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}))\cap\tau_{a_{n}}\left[\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\,\right]\cap\tau_{b}\left[\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\,\right]=\{\tau_{a_{n}}(\xi)\}.

Hence,
|\xi-T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a})|<\delta
and

\displaystyle|Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})-z|

\displaystyle=\left|Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})-Q_{n}(T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a});\mathbf{a})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{|\xi-T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a})|}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}\left|1-\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})\right|\left|1-\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}Q_{n}(T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a});\mathbf{a})\right|}

\displaystyle<\frac{\delta}{\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|

\displaystyle\text{(by }Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\text{)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho_{2}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|.

Then, for all
0<r<\frac{\rho_{2}}{2}
, we have
\mathbb{D}(z;r|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)\subseteq\mathbb{D}(Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a});\rho_{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|)
. The result follows from Lemma
6.6
after picking
\rho_{3}=\frac{\rho_{2}}{3}
.

Remark 6.13
.

Our choice of
\rho_{3}
implies that if
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
\xi\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})\cup\mathsf{Esq}
is such that
Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})\in\Fr(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))
, then

Q_{n}\left(\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})\cup\mathsf{Esq};\mathbf{a})\cap\mathbb{D}(Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a});\rho_{3}\right)=\{Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})\}.

6.1.5.
Proof of Lemma
6.8

Let
n
,
\mathbf{a}
, and
z
be as in the statement. Let
k\in\{0,1\ldots,n-1\}
be such that

z\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\cap\Fr(\mathcal{C}_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},a_{k+1})).

As before, we interpret
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\epsilon)=\mathfrak{F}
and
T_{0}(\,\cdot\,:\epsilon)
as the identity map. Let us first show that

(24)

w\colon=\iota T_{k}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))\in\Fr(\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}]).

For clarity, we discuss the conclusion on an example. When
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)
, the set
\iota[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})]
is the one depicted in Figure
10
. Then, the inclusion (
24
) means that
w
belongs to one of the dashed lines.

Figure 10.
The set
\iota[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)]
.

To show (
24
), note that

w\in\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right]\cap\iota T_{k}\left(\Fr\left(\mathcal{C}_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},a_{k+1})\right);(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right).

Then, in view of

\displaystyle\iota T_{k}\left(\mathcal{C}_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},a_{k+1}));(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\cap\mathcal{C}_{1}(a_{k+1})\right]

\displaystyle=\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right]\cap\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\iota[\mathfrak{F}]

\displaystyle=\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right]\cap\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}],

we have

w\in\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right]\cap\Fr\left(\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right]\cap\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}]\right).

From here, (
24
) is easily deduced.
Let us further assume that
k\leq n-2
. Take
0<\tilde{\rho}<\rho_{3}
, we impose later further restrictions on
\tilde{\rho}
. For
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
as in Proposition
5.15
, put

\delta\colon=\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{2}}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}.

We now consider two cases depending on how far is
w
from the boundary of
\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right]
. More precisely, we first assume that

(25)

\mathbb{D}\left(w;\frac{\delta\tilde{\rho}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|\right)\subseteq\iota\left[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right].

By
k\leq n-2
and Proposition
6.2
we have
w\not\in\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathsf{Esq}]
, so Remark
6.13
tells us that the set

\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cap\mathbb{D}\left(w;\frac{\delta\tilde{\rho}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|\right)

is contained in at most three sets of the form
\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(\mathbf{b})]
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n-k-2)
. Moreover, there is a single
b\in\mathscr{D}
with
|b-a_{k+1}|=1
and

\mathbb{D}\left(w;\frac{\delta\rho_{3}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|\right)\subseteq\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cup\tau_{b}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}].

By Lemma
6.7
and Subsection
5.3
, the previous disc is contained in the closure of at most six integral translates of closed regular cylinders of level
n-k-2
.

Now assume that (
25
) fails. Then, there is some
\xi\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})\cup\mathsf{Esq}
such that

\tau_{a_{k}}(\xi)\in\Fr\left(\iota(\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))\right)\cap\mathbb{D}\left(w;\frac{\delta\tilde{\rho}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|\right).

Using
w=\iota T_{k}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}))
and
\tau_{-a_{k+1}}(w)=T_{k+1}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1}))
, we get

\left|T_{k+1}\left(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right)-\xi\right|<\frac{\delta\tilde{\rho}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|.

Therefore, writing
\xi^{\prime}=Q_{k+1}(\xi;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1}))
, we obtain

\displaystyle|z-\xi^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=|Q_{k+1}\left(T_{k+1}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1});(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right)-Q_{k+1}(\xi;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1}))|

\displaystyle<\frac{\left|T_{k+1}\left(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right)-\xi\right|}{|q_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})|^{2}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle<\frac{\left|\mathcal{C}_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right|}{\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}\,\left|T_{k+1}\left(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right)-\xi\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta\tilde{\rho}}{2\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right||\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{2\kappa_{2}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\right||\mathcal{C}_{n-k-2}(a_{k+2},\ldots,a_{n})|

\displaystyle<\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|.

As a consequence, if
\rho_{4}:=\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{2}
, we have

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{4}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\left(Q_{k+1}(\xi;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1}));\tilde{\rho}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right).

Next, we explain why we can choose
\tilde{\rho}
so small that the larger disc in the previous inclusion is contained in the interior of at most six regular cylinders of level
k+1
. For concreteness, assume
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})=\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{1}(1+i)
; the other options are treated similarly. First, if
\tau_{a_{k}}(\xi)
is in one of the arches in
\Fr(\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})])
(see Figure
10
), then
\iota\tau_{a_{k}}(\xi)
belongs to one of the line segments in
\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{1}(1+i))
(see Figure
4
), we apply Lemma
6.7
and the proof is complete. If
\tau_{a_{k}}(\xi)
belongs to one of the rays in
\Fr(\iota[\mathfrak{F}_{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})])
, then
\iota\tau_{a_{k}}(\xi)
belongs to one of the arches in
\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{1}(1+i))
and, thus,

\iota\tau_{a_{k-1}}\iota\tau_{a_{k}}(\xi)=Q_{2}(\xi;(a_{k-1},a_{k}))\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F}).

To see it, apply
\tau_{-(1+i)}\iota
on
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(1+i)
and refer to Figure
2
. From this point, we can conclude after Lemma
6.7
.

The proof for
k=n-1
is similar. In fact, we only used
k\leq n-2
to avoid the possibility
\xi\in\mathsf{Esq}
. However, this situation is easier to deal with. We leave the details to the reader.

6.1.6.
Proof of Lemma
6.9

For
\rho_{4}>0
as in Lemma
6.8
, define

\rho_{5}\colon=\frac{\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}{2\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}\,\frac{\rho_{4}}{2}.

Let
n
,
\mathbf{a}
, and
z
be as in the statement. We may show as before that if

(26)

\mathbb{D}\left(T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a});\frac{\rho_{5}}{2}\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}\right)\subseteq\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=T_{n}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a});\mathbf{a}\right),

then

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{5}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a}).

When (
26
) is false, there is some
\xi\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
such that

\xi\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))\quad\text{ and }\quad|\xi-T_{n}(z;\mathbf{a})|<\frac{\rho_{5}}{2}\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}.

Then, as in the proof of Lemma
6.8
, we get

|z-Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a})|<\frac{\rho_{5}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|,

so

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\frac{\rho_{5}}{2}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\left(Q_{n}(\xi;\mathbf{a});\rho_{5}|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\right).

The result now follows from Lemma
6.8
.

We highlight two important consequences of the proofs of lemmas
6.5
and
6.6
. First, two neighboring cylinders have a similar diameter (Corollary
6.14
). Second, the partial quotients determining two neighboring cylinders are of similar size (Corollary
6.15
).
The details are left to the reader.

Corollary 6.14
.

There is a constant
\kappa_{3}>1
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, all
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and all
\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})

we have

\frac{1}{\kappa_{3}}\leq\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|}{|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{b})|}\leq\kappa_{3}.

Corollary 6.15
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, if
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathsf{Vec}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
then

\|a_{j}\|-1\leq\|b_{j}\|\leq\|a_{j}\|+1\;\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

6.2.
Second geometric construction

We dedicate this section to the proof of Theorem
6.16
. It counts how many sets of a certain form are intersected by discs with small radius. More precisely, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
0<\mathcal{l}<\mathcal{u}
, define

S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\colon=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{l}\leq\|d\|\leq\mathcal{u}\\
\mathbf{a}d\in\mathsf{R}(n+1)\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}d).

Theorem 6.16
.

There is a constant
\rho>0
with the following property: for any two natural numbers
\mathcal{l}
and
\mathcal{u}
such that
33\leq\mathcal{l}<\mathcal{u}
and
\frac{27}{100}<\frac{\mathcal{l}}{\mathcal{u}}<\frac{3}{4}
, any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and any
z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
, we have

\#\left\{\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n):\mathbb{D}(z;\rho|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|)\cap S_{n}(\mathbf{c};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\neq\varnothing\right\}\leq 2.

We introduce two types of sets underpinning our arguments in this section.

Definition 6.17
.

For any positive real numbers
0<L<U
, define

\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U)\colon=\Cl\left(\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:U^{-1}\leq\|\iota(z)\|\leq L^{-1}\right\}\right).

For each
R>0
, put

\mathfrak{L}(R)\colon=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\|\iota(z)\|=R^{-1}\right\}.

The next properties follow immediately from the definition of
\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U)
and
\mathfrak{L}(R)
.

Proposition 6.18
.

The next assertions hold:

i.

For every
R>0
, we have
R\,\mathfrak{L}(1)=\mathfrak{L}(R)
.

ii.

For all
0<L<U
, we have

\Fr(\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U))=\mathfrak{L}(L)\cup\mathfrak{L}(U).

iii.

For all
R>0
, we have

\mathfrak{L}(R)=\Fr\left(\mathbb{D}\left(\frac{R}{2};\frac{R}{2}\right)\cup\mathbb{D}\left(i\frac{R}{2};\frac{R}{2}\right)\cup\mathbb{D}\left(-\frac{R}{2};\frac{R}{2}\right)\cup\mathbb{D}\left(-i\frac{R}{2};\frac{R}{2}\right)\right).

iv.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq\mathcal{l}<\mathcal{u}
, we have

S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})=Q_{n}\left(\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap\mathfrak{Treb}\left((\mathcal{u}+\tfrac{1}{2})^{-1},\left(\mathcal{l}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{-1}\right)\right);\mathbf{a}\right).

We consider cases depending on the form of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
in the proof of Theorem
6.16
:

Case I
:
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}},\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
.

Case II
:
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2),\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
.

6.2.1.
Case I

In short, we first note that the choice of parameters yields

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{l}\leq\|d\|\leq\mathcal{u}\\
d\in\mathscr{D}\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d)\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a}).

Then, we apply the map
Q_{n}(\,\cdot\,;\mathbf{a})
on the sets
\mathfrak{Treb}(\frac{L}{2},2U)
and
\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U)
and conclude. We divide the full argument into lemmas
6.19
,
6.20
, and
6.21
.

Lemma 6.19
.

There is an absolute constant
\kappa^{\prime}>0
such that for all
R>0
we have

\dist\left(\mathfrak{L}(R),\mathfrak{L}(2R)\right)=\kappa^{\prime}R.

Proof.

The compact sets
\mathfrak{L}(1)
and
\mathfrak{L}(\frac{1}{2})
are disjoint, then
\kappa^{\prime}\colon=2\dist\left(\mathfrak{L}(1),\mathfrak{L}(\frac{1}{2})\right)>0
. These sets are depicted in Figure
11
. The lemma follows from Proposition
6.18
.
i
, because for all
R>0
the map
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
,
z\mapsto Rz
is a similarity. It is not hard to show that
\kappa^{\prime}=\sqrt{5}-1
, but we do not need the exact value.

Figure 11.
The sets
\mathfrak{L}(1)
and
\mathfrak{L}(\frac{1}{2})
.

∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
R\in(0,\tfrac{1}{8})
be arbitrary, and
\kappa^{\prime}>0
as in Lemma
6.19
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa^{\prime}R}{(1+2R)(1+4R)}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(R);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(2R);\mathbf{a})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\kappa^{\prime}R}{(1-2R)(1-4R)}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}.

Proof.

Write
q_{n}=q_{n}(\mathbf{a})
and
q_{n-1}=q_{n-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})
. If
\alpha\in\mathfrak{L}(R)
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{L}(2R)
, then

\displaystyle\left|Q_{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{a})-Q_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{a})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\alpha}{q_{n}^{2}\left(1-\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}\alpha\right)}\,-\,\frac{\beta}{q_{n}^{2}\left(1-\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}\beta\right)}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|q_{n}|^{2}}\frac{|\alpha-\beta|}{\left|\left(1-\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}\alpha\right)\left(1-\frac{q_{n-1}}{q_{n}}\beta\right)\right|}.

So, in view of
|\alpha|\leq R
,
|\beta|\leq 2R
and
|q_{n-1}|<|q_{n}|
, we have

\frac{1}{|q_{n}|^{2}}\frac{|\alpha-\beta|}{\left|\left(1+R\right)\left(1+2R\right)\right|}\leq\left|Q_{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{a})-Q_{n}(\beta;\mathbf{a})\right|\leq\frac{1}{|q_{n}|^{2}}\frac{|\alpha-\beta|}{\left|\left(1-R\right)\left(1-2R\right)\right|}.

The result now follows from Lemma
6.19
.
∎

Lemma 6.21
.

There is a constant
\rho_{1}^{\prime}>0
such that for every
0<L<U<\frac{1}{32}
satisfying
\frac{1}{4}<\frac{L}{U}<\frac{3}{4}
, all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, and
z\in Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U);\mathbf{d}\right)
, we have

(27)

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{1}^{\prime}\frac{U}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{d})|^{2}}\right)\subseteq Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right);\mathbf{d}\right).

Proof.

Let
L,U>0
be as in the statement. From

\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U)\subseteq\inte\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right)\right),

we get

Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U);\mathbf{a}\right)\subseteq\inte\left(Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\right).

It is, thus, enough to find some
\rho_{1}^{\prime}>0
such that

\dist\left(\Fr\left(Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U);\mathbf{a}\right)\right),\Fr\left(Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\right)\geq\rho_{1}^{\prime}\frac{U}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}.

Proposition
6.18
.
iv
implies

\Fr\left(Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U);\mathbf{a}\right)\right)=Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(L);\mathbf{a})\cup Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(U);\mathbf{a}),

and

\Fr\left(Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\right)=Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{L}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\cup Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(2U);\mathbf{a}).

We argue as in the proof of Lemma
6.20
to conclude

\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(U);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(2U);\mathbf{a})\right)\leq\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(U);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{L}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\right)

and

\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(L);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{L}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)\right)\right)\leq\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(L);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(2U);\mathbf{a})\right).

Hence, the result will follow after we prove that

(28)

\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(U);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(2U);\mathbf{a})\right)\geq\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(L);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{L}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\right)

and show the existence of some
\rho_{1}^{\prime}>0
for which

(29)

\dist\left(Q_{n}(\mathfrak{L}(L);\mathbf{a}),Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{L}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right);\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\geq\rho_{1}^{\prime}\frac{U}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}.

We first check (
28
). The assumptions on
L
and
U
lead us to

\frac{(1-L)(1-2L)}{(1+U)(1+2U)}>\frac{(1-U)(1-2U)}{(1+U)(1+2U)}>\frac{3}{4}>\frac{L}{U}.

So,

\kappa^{\prime}\frac{L}{(1-L)(1-2L)}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}<\kappa^{\prime}\frac{U}{(1+U)(1+2U)}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}

and we get (
28
) by Lemma
6.20
.

In order to get (
29
), first observe that
(1+L)(1+2L)\leq 4
, so

\frac{L}{U}\geq\frac{1}{4}\geq\frac{1}{16}(1+L)(1+2L)\;\text{ and }\;\frac{L}{(1+L)(1+2L)}\geq\frac{U}{16}.

The result is now a consequence of Lemma
6.20
.
∎

Remark 6.22
.

By Proposition
5.15
.
i
, we may shrink
\rho_{1}^{\prime}>0
so that we can replace the last inclusion in Lemma
6.20
with

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{1}U|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{d})|\right)\subseteq Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right);\mathbf{d}\right).

Remark 6.23
.

We will use Lemma
6.21
for Case II as well. Assume for a moment that
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{d})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2),\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
. Then, Lemma
6.21
implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{1}U|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{d})|\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\subseteq Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right);\mathbf{d}\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle=Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\frac{L}{2},2U\right)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{d});\mathbf{d}\right).

In this case, the disc
\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{1}U|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{d})|\right)
might certainly not be contained in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{d})
. However, it intersects at most one side of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{d})
.

Proof of Theorem
6.16
: Case I.

Let
\mathcal{l}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 33}
and
\mathcal{u}\in\mathbb{N}
be as in the statement and put

L=\left(\mathcal{u}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}\;\text{ and }\;U=\left(\mathcal{l}-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}.

Then, we have

\frac{1}{4}<\frac{L}{U}<\frac{3}{4}\;\text{ and }\;U<\frac{1}{32}.

If
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}},\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
, then
\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U)\subseteq\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
and, by Proposition
6.18
.
iv
,

S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})=Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(L,U);\mathbf{a}\right).

We conclude the proof of this case by choosing
\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{1}^{\prime}
as in Lemma
6.21
.
∎

6.2.2.
Case II

We now assume
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}}(2),\,\overline{\mathfrak{F}}(1+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
.

Proposition 6.24
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
4<\mathcal{l}<\mathcal{u}
. Every
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
satisfies

\#\left\{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n):z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\right\}\leq 2.

Proof.

Take any
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
and assume there are three different words
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that

z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{n}(\mathbf{c};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}).

Let
k
be the largest non-negative integer such that

\prefi(\mathbf{a};k)=\prefi(\mathbf{b};k)=\prefi(\mathbf{c};k).

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
a_{k+1}\neq b_{k+1}
. We must have either
c_{k+1}=a_{k+1}
or
c_{k+1}=b_{k+1}
. Otherwise, we would have

\iota T_{k}\left(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\right)\in\tau_{a_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\tau_{b_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}]\cap\tau_{c_{k+1}}[\mathfrak{F}]

and, hence,
T_{k+1}(z;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},a_{k+1}))\in\mathsf{Esq}
. However, since
k+1\leq n
, Proposition
6.2
would forbid
z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
. Assume that
c_{k+1}=b_{k+1}
, so
c_{k+1}\neq a_{k+1}
. Let
j\in\mathbb{N}
be the least integer such that
b_{j+k}\neq c_{j+k}
, so
k+j\leq n
. Then,
z^{\prime}=T_{k+j-1}(z;(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k+j-1}))
satisfies

z^{\prime}\in\Fr\left(\mathfrak{F}_{k+j-1}(b_{1},\ldots,\ldots,b_{k+j-1})\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(b_{k+j})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(c_{k+j}).

The inclusion
\Fr\left(\mathfrak{F}_{k+j-1}(b_{1},\ldots,\ldots,b_{k+j-1})\right)
follows from
z^{\prime}\in\Fr\left(\Fr\left(\mathfrak{F}_{k}(b_{1},\ldots,\ldots,b_{k})\right)\right)
. Therefore, in view of Lemma
6.12
.
i
, we conclude

T_{k+j}(z;(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k+j}))\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}(\zeta_{1})\cup\mathsf{Esq}.

However, this would contradict
z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
.
(thepartialquotientswouldbesmall)
.
∎

Proposition 6.25
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
be arbitrary, and let
c\in\mathscr{D}
be such that
\mathbf{a}c\in\mathsf{R}(n+1)
. If
c\in\mathscr{D}
satisfies
\mathbf{a}c\in\mathsf{R}(n+1)
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(c)\setminus\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{k}(\mathbf{a})\neq\varnothing
implies
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
.

Proof.

Simply verify four possible forms of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\mod\mathsf{Rot}
.
∎

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{l}<\mathcal{u}
, be given. Assume that

\left(\ell-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}<\frac{1}{10}\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{4}<\frac{\mathcal{l}-\frac{1}{2}}{\mathcal{u}+\frac{1}{2}}<\frac{3}{4}.

There exists a number
\rho_{2}^{\prime}>0
with the following property: for every
k,j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(k)
,
\mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}\in\mathsf{R}(j)
such that
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d},\mathbf{a}\mathbf{e}\in\mathsf{R}(k+j)
, and

z\in S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{e};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}),

then we have

\mathbb{D}\left(T_{k}(z;\mathbf{a});\frac{\rho_{2}}{\left(\mathcal{l}-\frac{1}{2}\right)|q_{j}(\mathbf{d})|^{2}}\right)\subseteq S_{j}\left(\mathbf{d};\left\lfloor\frac{\mathcal{l}-1}{2}\right\rfloor,2(\mathcal{u}+1)\right)\cup S_{j}\left(\mathbf{e};\left\lfloor\frac{\mathcal{l}-1}{2}\right\rfloor,2(\mathcal{u}+1)\right).

Proof.

The proof is divided into two steps. First, we show that the cylinders
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{j})
are a reflection of each other. Then, we apply Remark
6.23
to obtain the existence of
\rho_{2}^{\prime}>0
.

Step I.
Let us show that

(30)

\mathsf{mir}\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})\right]=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{j})\quad\text{ for some }\mathsf{mir}\in\{\mathsf{mir}_{1},\mathsf{mir}_{2}\}.

Define
w\colon=T_{k}(z;\mathbf{a})
, then

(31)

w\in S_{j}(\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{j}(\mathbf{e};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}).

Pick
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}
such that

(32)

\mathcal{l}\leq\|b\|\leq\mathcal{u},\quad\mathcal{l}\leq\|b^{\prime}\|\leq\mathcal{u},\quad z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k+j+1}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d}b)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k+j+1}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d}b^{\prime}).

From
\iota(w)\in\tau_{e_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]\cap\tau_{d_{1}}[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}]
we conclude that
1\leq|e_{1}-d_{1}|\leq\sqrt{2}
. If we had
|e_{1}-d_{1}|=\sqrt{2}
, we would have
w\in\mathsf{Esq}
. However, by Proposition
6.2
, this contradicts (
31
); therefore, we must have
|d_{1}-e_{1}|=1
. Without loss of generality, assume that
e_{1}=d_{1}+1
.
We claim that

(33)

\tau_{d_{1}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(d_{2})\right]\cup\tau_{e_{1}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(e_{2})\right]\subseteq\iota\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right].

Certainly, by Lemma
6.12
.
i
, Proposition
6.25
and
e_{1}=d_{1}+1
, if we had
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{1}(d_{2})\setminus\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\neq\varnothing
, we would have
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}d_{1})=\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{1}(1+i)
and
\iota(w)=\tau_{d_{1}}\,\mathsf{mir}_{1}(\zeta_{1})
. The proof of Proposition
6.10
would then yield

\|d_{2}\|=\ldots=\|d_{j}\|=\|b\|=2,

which contradicts (
31
) and
\mathcal{l}\geq 10
. The same argument shows
\tau_{e_{1}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(e_{2})\right]\subseteq\iota\left[\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right]
. We, thus, conclude (
33
).

Now, we show that
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})
is the single regular cylinder of level
j-1
whose closure contains
T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1})
. Assume there was some
(d_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,d_{j}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}(j-1)
different from
(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})
such that

T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1})\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,d_{j}^{\prime})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j}).

Then, since
T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1})\in\Fr(\mathfrak{F})
, an application of Proposition
6.12
.
i
would forbid

T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1})\in S_{j-1}((d_{2},\ldots,d_{j});\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}).

Similarly, we may show that
T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}e_{1})
belongs to the closure of a single regular cylinder of level
j-1
; namely,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{j})
.

Observe that, using our assumption
e_{1}=d_{1}+1
, we have

\mathsf{mir}_{1}\left(T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1})\right)=T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}e_{1}).

As a consequence,
T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}e_{1})\in\mathsf{mir}_{1}\left[\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})\right]
and, because of the uniqueness of
(e_{2},\ldots,e_{j})
, we conclude (
30
).

Step II.
Next, for
\mathcal{l}^{\prime}=\lfloor\frac{\ell-1}{2}\rfloor
and
\mathcal{u}^{\prime}=2(\mathcal{u}+1)
, we show that there are absolute constant
\rho_{2}^{\prime}>0
such that the disc

(34)

\mathbb{D}\left(\iota(w);\frac{\rho_{2}^{\prime}}{\left(\mathcal{l}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\right)|q_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})|^{2}}\right)

is contained in

(35)

\tau_{d_{1}}\left[Q_{j-1}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime});(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j-1}\right)\right]\cup\tau_{e_{1}}\left[Q_{j}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}(\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime});(e_{2},\ldots,e_{j-1}\right)\right].

To this end, note that the side of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})
containing
T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1})
is a segment of
\Fr(\mathfrak{F})
. Then, by Remark
6.23
, the left half of the disc

\mathbb{D}\left(T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}d_{1});\frac{\rho_{1}}{\left(\mathcal{l}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})\right|\right)

is contained in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})
. Similarly, the right half of the disc

\mathbb{D}\left(T_{k+1}(z;\mathbf{a}e_{1});\frac{\rho_{1}}{\left(\mathcal{l}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{j})\right|\right)

is contained in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{j})
. Therefore, the disc in (
34
) is contained in the set in (
35
) after taking an absolutely small
\rho_{2}^{\prime}
(see Proposition
5.15
).
∎

As in the proof of Lemma
6.19
, we may show the existence of some absolute constant
\rho_{3}^{\prime}>0
such that for all
0<L<U<\frac{1}{8}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
we have

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{3}^{\prime}|S_{k+j}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|\right)\subseteq S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{e};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})\cap S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime}).

This observation combined with Remark
6.23
and Proposition
6.26
yield the next result.

Proposition 6.27
.

There is some
\rho_{4}^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{e}
as in Proposition
6.26
, every
z\in S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{e};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
verifies

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{4}^{\prime}|S_{k+j}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|\right)\subseteq S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{e};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})\cap S_{j+k}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{d};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime}).

Proof of Theorem
6.16
: Case II.

First, we assume that there are two different
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
for which

z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u}).

Take
\rho_{4}^{\prime}>0
as in Proposition
6.27
, so

(36)

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\rho_{4}^{\prime}|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|\right)\subseteq S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})\cup S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime}).

Next, let us show that

(37)

\left\{\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n):S_{n}(\mathbf{c};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{3}^{\prime}|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|)\right\}\subseteq\{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\}.

If this was not true, then there would be some
w\in\mathbb{D}(z;2\rho|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|)
and
\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n)\setminus\{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\}
such that
w\in S_{n}(\mathbf{c};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
. Take
c\in\mathscr{D}
with
\mathcal{l}\leq\|c\|\leq\mathcal{u}
and
w\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{c}c)
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{c}c)=\Cl(\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(\mathbf{c}c))
, we would have

\mathbb{D}(z;\rho_{4}^{\prime}|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|)\cap\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(\mathbf{c}c)\neq\varnothing;

however, since
\mathbf{c}\neq\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{c}\neq\mathbf{b}
, we know that

\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{c})\cap\left(S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})\cup S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})\right)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{c})\cap\left(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cup\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\right)=\varnothing,

which is a contradiction. Therefore, (
37
) holds and the theorem has been proven in this subcase.

Now assume that
\mathbf{a}
is the only element
\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
for which
z\in S_{n}(\mathbf{c};\ell,\mathcal{u})
holds. If the disc
\mathbb{D}\left(z;\frac{\rho_{4}^{\prime}}{2}|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|\right)
only intersects one set of the form
S_{n}(\mathbf{c};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
, we are done. Otherwise, there would be some
\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
,
\mathbf{a}\neq\mathbf{b}
, and some
w\in S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})\cap S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})
. We may, thus, use the previous subcase along with

\mathbb{D}\left(z;\frac{\rho_{4}^{\prime}}{2}|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|\right)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\left(w;\rho_{4}^{\prime}|S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l},\mathcal{u})|\right)\subseteq S_{n}(\mathbf{a};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})\cup S_{n}(\mathbf{b};\mathcal{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{u}^{\prime})

to conclude the theorem.
∎

7.
Approaching the irregular by the regular

So far, our discussion has revolved around regular numbers and their associated objects (cylinders, prototype sets, words). However, as noted before, there are uncountably many irregular numbers. Theorem
7.1
helps us understand why we can overlook irregular numbers in the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Theorem 7.1
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{Ir}(n)
, and
f\in\mathsf{Di}_{8}
, there is some
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that

f\left[\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right]\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})\quad\text{ and }\quad\|a_{j}\|=\|b_{j}\|\;\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Corollary 7.2
.

There is a single continuous extension
\overline{\Lambda}
of
\Lambda|_{\mathsf{R}}
to the closure
\overline{\mathsf{R}}
of
\mathsf{R}
. Moreover,
\overline{\Lambda}[\overline{\mathsf{R}}]=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\mathbb{Q}(i)
.

Using a slightly different notation, Theorem
7.1
and Corollary
7.2
are proven in
[
23
]
. Moreover, that paper provides a procedure to obtain
\mathbf{b}
. We, thus, omit the proofs and refer the reader to
[
23
, Subsection 7.2]
.

Remark 7.3
.

We can deal with irregular numbers in a different way. It is not hard to show that the set of irregular numbers has Hausdorff dimension
1
. To see this, note that an irregular cylinder is either a line segment or an arch and there are countably many of them. Hence, we may ignore them while proving Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.3
. The downside of this strategy is the loss of Corollary
7.2
.

8.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

We start the proof of Theorem
1.3
by showing the existence of the limit in the statement. Then, we divide the proof into two parts: the upper bound and the lower bound. For the upper bound, we use a natural covering argument. For the lower bound, we apply the Mass Distribution Principle (Lemma
8.16
below).

8.1.
The functions
g_{n}

The numbers
N_{1}\colon=11
and
\psi\colon=\sqrt{\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}}
will remain fixed throughout this section. Write

\forall M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 4}\qquad\mathscr{D}(M)\colon=\ \{a\in\mathscr{D}:\|a\|\leq M\}.

Definition 8.1
.

Given
B>1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
, define

\forall\rho\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\qquad g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A},B)\colon=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{\rho}.

When
\mathscr{A}
and
B
are fixed, we write
g_{n}(\rho)
instead of
g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A},B)
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Take
B>1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
, and let
\mathscr{A}
be either
\mathscr{E}
,
\mathscr{D}(M)
for some
M\geq 4
or
\mathscr{D}
.

i.

The function
\rho\mapsto g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A},B)
is non-increasing.

ii.

If
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is
\mathscr{E}
,
\mathscr{D}(M)
for some
M\geq 4
or
\mathscr{D}
, and
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
; then,
g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A},B)\leq g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A}^{\prime},B)
.

Proof.

i.

From
|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|>\psi^{n-1}\geq\psi^{10}>10
, we have
5\sqrt{2}<\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|
, hence
\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}<1
. The definition of
g_{n}
then tells us that
0<\rho<\rho^{\prime}
implies
g_{n}(\rho^{\prime};\mathscr{A},B)\leq g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A},B)
.

ii.

The proof is straightforward.

∎

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
B>1
, and
\mathscr{A}
equal to
\mathscr{E}
, or
\mathscr{D}(M)
for some
M\geq 4
, or
\mathscr{D}
, we define

s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\colon=\inf\{\rho>0:g_{n}(\rho;\mathscr{A},B)\leq 1\}.

For notational ease, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
B>1
,
\rho>0
, and
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(j)
, put

\tilde{h}_{j}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\colon=(B^{j}\|q_{j}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2})^{-\rho}.

The next estimates follow from Proposition
5.15
.
ii
.

Proposition 8.3
.

Take
B>1
. For all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)
,
\mathbf{b}\in\Omega(m)
such that
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in\Omega(m+n)
, and
\rho>0
, we have

\frac{1}{6^{2\rho}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{b};\rho)\leq\tilde{h}_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b};\rho)\leq(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{b};\rho).

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be either
\mathscr{D}
,
\mathscr{D}(M)
for some
M\geq 4
, or
\mathscr{E}
, and
B>1
. For every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho>0
, we have

g_{n+m}(\rho)\leq g_{n}(\rho)g_{m}(\rho).

Proof.

For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho>0
, we have

\displaystyle g_{n+m}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)}(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{n+m}(\mathbf{c};\rho)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\mathbf{b}\in\mathscr{A}^{m}\cap\mathsf{R}(m)\end{subarray}}(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{a};\rho)(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{b};\rho)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)}(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\right)\left(\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathscr{A}^{m}\cap\mathsf{R}(m)}(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{b};\rho)\right)

\displaystyle=g_{n}(\rho)g_{m}(\rho).

∎

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be either
\mathscr{D}
,
\mathscr{D}(M)
for some
M\geq 4
, or
\mathscr{E}
, and
B>1
. Write
\kappa_{4}\colon=\frac{3\sqrt{2}+1}{\sqrt{2}-1}
. For every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho>0
, the next estimate holds:

\frac{1}{1800^{\rho}+20(3600\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}}g_{n}(\rho)g_{m}(\rho)\leq g_{n+m}(\rho).

Proof.

Take any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho>0
. Then,

\displaystyle g_{m}(\rho)g_{n}(\rho)

\displaystyle=g_{m}(\rho)(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)

(38)

\displaystyle=g_{m}(\rho)(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\geq 3\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)+g_{m}(\rho)(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho).

We now bound separately each of the two addends in (
38
). By Proposition
8.3
, the first term can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\geq 3\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\right)g_{m}(\rho)

\displaystyle=(5\sqrt{2})^{4\rho}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\geq 3\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\right)\left(\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathscr{A}^{m}\cap\mathsf{R}(m)}\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{b};\rho)\right)

\displaystyle\leq(5\sqrt{2})^{4\rho}6^{2\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{AF}(n)\\
\mathbf{b}\in\mathscr{A}^{m}\cap\mathsf{R}(m)\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n+m}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b};\rho)

\displaystyle\leq(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}6^{2\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{A}^{n+m}\cap\mathsf{R}(n+m)(\mathscr{A})}(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{n+m}(\mathbf{c};\rho);

that is

(39)

(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\geq 3\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\right)g_{m}(\rho)\leq 1800^{\rho}g_{n+m}(\rho).

We need a more intricate argument for the second term in (
38
). For each
\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)
such that
\|a_{n}\|\leq 2
, choose
d(\mathbf{a})\in\mathscr{D}
satisfying

\Pm(d(\mathbf{a}))=\|d(\mathbf{a})\|=3\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a})\colon=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1},d)\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{F}(n),

(see Proposition
5.9
). Then, we have

\left|\frac{q_{n}(\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a}))}{q_{n}(\mathbf{a})}\right|=\left|\frac{d(\mathbf{a})q_{n-1}(\mathbf{a})+q_{n-2}(\mathbf{a})}{a_{n}q_{n-1}(\mathbf{a})+q_{n-2}(\mathbf{a})}\right|=\left|\frac{d(\mathbf{a})+\frac{q_{n-2}(\mathbf{a})}{q_{n-1}(\mathbf{a})}}{a_{n}+\frac{q_{n-2}(\mathbf{a})}{q_{n-1}(\mathbf{a})}}\right|\leq\frac{|d(\mathbf{a})|+1}{|a_{n}|-1}\leq\frac{3\sqrt{2}+1}{\sqrt{2}-1}=\kappa_{4},

which implies

\|q_{n}(\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a}))\|\leq|q_{n}(\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a}))|\leq\kappa_{4}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\kappa_{4}\sqrt{2}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|.

Hence

(40)

\frac{1}{(B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2})^{\rho}}\leq\frac{(2\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}}{(B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a}))\|^{2})^{\rho}}.

The function
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a})
might not be injective, but it is at most twenty-to-one. Indeed, for any
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-1},b_{n})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{F}(n)
such that
\Pm(b_{n})=\|b_{n}\|=3
, the set of words
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)
satisfying
\|a_{n}\|\leq 2
and
\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a})=\mathbf{b}
is contained in the set

\{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n):(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-1}),\|a_{n}\|\leq 2\},

which has at most
20
elements, because
\#\{a\in\mathscr{D}:\|a\|\leq 2\}=20
. This observation and (
40
) give

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)

\displaystyle\leq(2\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{a});\rho)

\displaystyle\leq 20(2\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\Pm(c_{n})=\|c_{n}\|=3\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{c};\rho)

\displaystyle\leq 20(2\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{F}(n)}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{c};\rho).

Then, by Proposition
8.3
, we have

\displaystyle(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{R}(n)\\
\|a_{n}\|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\right)g_{m}(\rho)

\displaystyle\leq(5\sqrt{2})^{4\rho}20(2\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{n}\cap\mathsf{F}(n)}\tilde{h}_{n}(\mathbf{a};\rho)\right)\left(\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathscr{A}^{m}\cap\mathsf{R}(m)}\tilde{h}_{m}(\mathbf{b};\rho)\right)

\displaystyle\leq(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}20(2\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}6^{2\rho}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{A}^{n+m}\cap\mathsf{R}(n+m)}(5\sqrt{2})^{2\rho}\tilde{h}_{n+m}(\mathbf{c};\rho)

\displaystyle=20(3600\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\rho}g_{n+m}(\rho),

and the proof is done.
∎

8.2.
The dimensional number
s_{B}

In this subsection, we give an alternative definition of the number
s_{B}
. After establishing some relevant properties, we show that this definition is equivalent to the one in (
2
).

Proposition 8.6
.

If
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
are coprime and
p<q
, then

\mathbb{N}_{\geq p(q-1)}\subseteq\{j\in\mathbb{N}:j=pk+tq\text{ for some }k,t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

Proof.

See
[
2
, Section 3. Problem 14]
.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
B>1
be arbitrary. If
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{D}
,
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{E}
, or
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{D}(M)
for some
M\geq 4
, then the following limit exists

s_{B}(\mathscr{A})\colon=\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A}).

Proof.

For clarity, we divide the proof into six steps.

i.

We show that for
n,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
and
r,k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(41)

s_{rn+tk,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq\max\{s_{rn,B}(\mathscr{A}),s_{tk,B}(\mathscr{A})\}\leq\max\{s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A}),s_{t,B}(\mathscr{A})\}.

To this end, take any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
. We obtain (
41
) from the stronger inequality

(42)

s_{m+n,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq\max\{s_{m,B}(\mathscr{A}),s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\}.

Take
\varepsilon>0
and
\rho=\max\{s_{m,B}(\mathscr{A}),s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\}+\varepsilon
. Then,
g_{m}(\rho)<1
,
g_{n}(\rho)<1
and, by Proposition
8.4
,
g_{n+m}(\rho)\leq g_{n}(\rho)g_{m}(\rho)<1
. Therefore,

s_{m+n,B}(\mathscr{A})<\max\{s_{m,B}(\mathscr{A}),s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\}+\varepsilon,

which proves (
42
).

ii.

Define

\overline{s}\colon=\limsup_{n\to\infty}s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A}).

We claim that

(43)

\#\{n\in\mathbb{N}:s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq\overline{s}\}=\infty.

If (
43
) were false, there would be some
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
satisfying

s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})<\overline{s}\;\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{0}}.

Define

\overline{r}\colon=\max\{s_{N_{0}+r,B}\colon r\in\{0,1,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}\}<\overline{s}.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2N_{0}}
, let
q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
and
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
be such that
n=qN_{0}+r
, then

\displaystyle s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})

\displaystyle=s_{(q-1)N_{0}+(N_{0}+r),B}(\mathscr{A})

\displaystyle\leq\max\{s_{(q-1)N_{0},B}(\mathscr{A}),s_{N_{0}+r,B}(\mathscr{A})\}

(by (
41
))

\displaystyle\leq\max\{s_{(q-1)N_{0},B}(\mathscr{A}),\overline{r}\}

\displaystyle<\overline{s}.

However, this leads us to the contradiction

\overline{s}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq\overline{r}<\overline{s},

so (
43
) must hold.

iii.

Observe that

(44)

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}(N_{1}+1)}\qquad s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq\max\{s_{N_{1},B}(\mathscr{D}),s_{N_{1}+1,B}(\mathscr{D})\}.

Certainly, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}(N_{1}+1)}
, Proposition
8.2
.
ii
, (
41
), and Proposition
8.6
give

s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq s_{n,B}(\mathscr{D})\leq\max\{s_{N_{1},B}(\mathscr{D}),s_{N_{1}+1,B}(\mathscr{D})\}.

iv.

Write

\hat{s}\colon=\max\{s_{N_{1},B}(\mathscr{D}),s_{N_{1}+1,B}(\mathscr{D})\},\quad\kappa(\hat{s})\colon=(1800^{\hat{s}}+20(3600\kappa_{4}^{2})^{\hat{s}})^{-1}.

We now show that

(45)

\forall\delta>0\qquad g_{n}(s_{jn,B}(\mathscr{A})+\delta)\leq\frac{1}{\kappa(\hat{s})^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

By Proposition
8.5
, we have

(46)

\forall\rho\in(0,\hat{s})\quad\forall m,n\in\mathbb{N}\qquad\kappa(\hat{s})g_{n}(\rho)g_{m}(\rho)\leq g_{n+m}(\rho).

Hence, if
0<\rho<\hat{s}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain
g_{jn}(\rho)\geq\kappa(\hat{s})^{j-1}g_{n}(\rho)^{j}
and

g_{n}(\rho)\leq\frac{1}{\kappa(\hat{s})^{1-\frac{1}{j}}}g_{jn}(\rho)^{\frac{1}{j}}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa(\hat{s})^{\frac{1}{2}}}g_{jn}(\rho)^{\frac{1}{j}}.

Using the definition of
s_{nj,B}(\mathscr{A})
, we conclude (
45
).

v.

Let us show that

(47)

\forall r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}(N_{1}+1)}\qquad s_{r,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq\overline{s}.

Assume that
r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}(N_{1}+1)}
satisfies
s_{r,B}(\mathscr{A})<\overline{s}
. Consider
\varepsilon\colon=\overline{s}-s_{r,B}(\mathscr{A})>0
. By (
43
), we may choose some
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that

s_{p,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq\overline{s}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left(\frac{B^{p}\psi^{p-1}}{5\sqrt{2}}\right)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}>\kappa(\hat{s})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Then, taking
\delta=\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}
in (
45
), we have

\displaystyle g_{p}\left(s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{p}\cap\mathsf{R}(p)}\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{2s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\varepsilon}}{(B^{p}\|q_{p}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2})^{s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{p}\psi^{p-1}}\right)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{A}^{p}\cap\mathsf{R}(p)}\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{2s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}{(B^{p}\|q_{p}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2})^{s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}

(by Proposition
4.2
.
ii
)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{p}\psi^{p-1}}\right)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}g_{p}\left(s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle<1.

Thus,

s_{p,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

and, by (
41
),

s_{p,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq\overline{s}=s_{r,B}(\mathscr{A})+\varepsilon\geq s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\varepsilon.

These inequalities imply

\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq s_{p,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq s_{rp,B}(\mathscr{A})+\varepsilon,\;\text{ so }\;0>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

In view of this contradiction, (
47
) must hold.

vi.

We conclude the result, because (
47
) yields

\liminf_{n\to\infty}s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\geq\overline{s}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A}).

∎

For any
p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
, write
s_{p,B}\colon=s_{p,B}(\mathscr{D})
and
s_{p,B}(M)\colon=s_{p,B}(\mathscr{D}(M))
for all
M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 4}
.

Lemma 8.8
.

If
p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
, then
\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}s_{p,B}(M)=s_{p,B}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.2
.
ii
, the map
M\mapsto s_{p,B}(M)
is non-decreasing for
M\geq 4
and bounded above by
s_{p,B}
. Then, the next limit exists:

t_{p,B}\colon=\lim_{M\to\infty}s_{p,B}(M)\leq s_{p,B}.

The definition of
s_{p,B}
and
p\geq N_{1}
give

\forall\rho\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\qquad g_{p}(\rho;\mathscr{D},B)=\lim_{M\to\infty}g_{p}(\rho;\mathscr{D}(M),B).

Take
\varepsilon>0
. Then, by
g_{p}(s_{p,B}-\varepsilon;\mathscr{D},B)>1
and the above limit, every sufficiently large
M
satisfies

g_{p}(s_{n,B}-\varepsilon,\mathscr{D}(M),B)>1,

so
s_{p,B}-\varepsilon\leq s_{p,B}(M)\leq t_{p,B}
and
s_{p,B}\leq t_{p,B}
.
∎

Write
s_{B}\colon=s_{B}(\mathscr{D})
and
s_{B}(M)\colon=s_{B}(\mathscr{D}(M))
for all
M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
.

Lemma 8.9
.

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}s_{B}(M)=s_{B}
.

Proof.

Take
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{N}(B,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
be such that

\kappa(\hat{s})^{\frac{1}{2}}<\left(\frac{B^{\widetilde{N}}\psi^{\widetilde{N}}}{5\sqrt{2}}\right)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}.

Consider any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq\widetilde{N}}
. As in part
v
in the proof of Lemma
8.7
, we conclude that if
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{D}
,
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{E}
, or
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{D}(M)
,
M\geq 4
, then every
m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq s_{mn,B}(\mathscr{A})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Moreover, (
41
) gives
s_{mn,B}(\mathscr{A})\leq s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})
, so

|s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})-s_{mn,B}(\mathscr{A})|<\varepsilon.

We let
m\to\infty
and use Lemma
8.7
to get

(48)

|s_{n,B}(\mathscr{A})-s_{B}(\mathscr{A})|<\varepsilon.

Also, by Lemma
8.8
, every sufficiently large
M
satisfies

(49)

|s_{n,B}(M)-s_{n,B}|<\varepsilon.

Inequalities (
48
) and (
49
) lead us to the desired conclusion:

|s_{B}(M)-s_{B}|\leq|s_{B}(M)-s_{n,B}(M)|+|s_{n,B}(M)-s_{n,B}|+|s_{n,B}-s_{B}|<3\varepsilon.

∎

For
B>1
and
\kappa_{1}
as in Proposition
5.15
.
i
, define

N_{2}\colon=\max\left\{N_{1},\frac{\log(50)-\log\pi-\log\kappa_{1}}{2\log B}+1\right\}.

Proposition 8.10
.

For any
B>1
and
M\geq 4
, we have

\sup\left\{s_{n,B}(M):n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{2}},M\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\leq 2.

Proof.

Take
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{2}}
. For any
M\geq 4
, we have
s_{n,B}(M)\leq s_{n,B}
by Proposition
8.2
. Moreover,
s_{n,B}<2
holds, because of

g_{n}(2;\mathscr{D},B)=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{2}=\frac{50}{B^{2n}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{1}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{4}}\leq\frac{50}{B^{2n}\kappa_{1}\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))\\
<1.

∎

The next estimate can be shown inductively on
n
.

Proposition 8.11
.

If
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, then

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(|a_{j}|-1)\leq\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}(|a_{j}|+1).

Lemma 8.12
.

The function
B\mapsto s_{B}
, mapping
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
to
\mathbb{R}
, has the following properties:

i.

it is continuous.

ii.

\displaystyle\lim_{B\to 1}s_{B}=2
,

iii.

\displaystyle\lim_{B\to\infty}s_{B}=1
,

Proof.

i.

Let
B_{0}>1
be given. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be arbitrary. Put

\delta_{1}\colon=\frac{1}{2}\left(B_{0}-B_{0}^{\frac{1}{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}}\right),\quad\delta_{2}\colon=\frac{1}{2}\left(B_{0}^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}-B_{0}\right),\quad\delta\colon=\min\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}

and

N\colon=\max\left\{N_{2},\;\frac{\log(5\sqrt{2})}{2\left(\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)\log(B_{0}-\delta)-\log B_{0}\right)},\;\frac{\log(5\sqrt{2})}{2\left(\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)\log B_{0}-\log(B_{0}+\delta)\right)}\right\}.

We chose the parameters for the next estimates to hold:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N}\qquad\left(\frac{B_{0}}{(B_{0}-\delta)^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}}\right)^{2n}<\frac{1}{5\sqrt{2}},\quad\left(\frac{B_{0}+\delta}{B_{0}^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}}\right)^{2n}<\frac{1}{5\sqrt{2}}.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N(B_{0},\delta)}
, we have

\displaystyle g_{n}\left(s_{n,B_{0}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2};\mathscr{D},B_{0}-\delta\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{(B_{0}-\delta)^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{n,B_{0}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}{(B_{0}-\delta)^{\frac{n\varepsilon}{2}}}\left(\frac{B_{0}}{B_{0}-\delta}\right)^{ns_{n,B_{0}}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B_{0}^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{n,B_{0}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}{(B_{0}-\delta)^{\frac{n\varepsilon}{2}}}\left(\frac{B_{0}}{B_{0}-\delta}\right)^{ns_{n,B_{0}}}g_{n}(s_{n,B};\mathscr{D},B_{0})

\displaystyle\leq 5\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{B_{0}}{(B_{0}-\delta)^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}}\right)^{2n}

\displaystyle<1,

and, thus,
s_{n,B_{0}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\geq s_{n,B_{0}-\delta}\geq s_{n,B_{0}}
. Lastly, we obtain
s_{n,B_{0}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\leq s_{n,B_{0}+\delta}\leq s_{n,B_{0}}
from

\displaystyle g_{n}\left(s_{n,B_{0}+\delta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2};\mathscr{D},B_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B_{0}^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{n,B_{0}+\delta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}{B_{0}^{\frac{n\varepsilon}{2}}}\left(\frac{B_{0}+\delta}{B_{0}}\right)^{ns_{n,B_{0}+\delta}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{(B_{0}+\delta)^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{n,B_{0}+\delta}}

\displaystyle<5\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{B_{0}+\delta}{B_{0}^{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}}\right)^{2n}\qquad\qquad(\text{by }0<\varepsilon<1\text{ and }s_{n,B_{0}}<2)

\displaystyle<1.

The result is now a consequence of Proposition
8.7
.

ii.

Consider
0<\varepsilon<2
and let
B
be such that
1<B^{2}<\psi^{\varepsilon}
. If
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

n\geq\max\left\{N_{2},\frac{2\varepsilon\log(\psi)+\log\pi}{\varepsilon\log\psi-2\log B}\right\},

then

\displaystyle g_{n}(2-\varepsilon;\mathscr{D},B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{2-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(5\sqrt{2})^{2-\varepsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{1}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{2-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2\varepsilon}}{B^{2n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(2\psi)^{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\psi^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}}\right)^{n}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{1}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(2\psi)^{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\psi^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}}\right)^{n}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(2\psi)^{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\psi^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}}\right)^{n}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{1}{\pi}\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(2\psi)^{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\psi^{\varepsilon}}{B^{2}}\right)^{n}\frac{1}{\pi}

\displaystyle>1.

Hence,
2-\varepsilon<s_{n,B}<2
(by Proposition
8.8
) and we conclude that
s_{n,B}\to 2
as
B\to 1
.

iii.

Take any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
. First, note that
s_{n,B}\geq 1
, since

\displaystyle g_{n}(1,\mathscr{D},B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(|a_{j}|+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{E}^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(|a_{j}|+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}}\left(\sum_{a\in\mathscr{E}}\frac{1}{(|a|+1)^{2}}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(5\sqrt{2})}{2^{n}B^{n}}\left(\sum_{a\in\mathscr{E}}\frac{1}{|a|^{2}}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=\infty.

Take
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and choose
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
so large that
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N}
implies

200\left(\sum_{|a|\geq\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{(|a_{j}|-1)^{2+2\varepsilon}}\right)^{n}<\left(\sum_{|a|\geq\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{(|a_{j}|-1)^{2+\varepsilon}}\right)^{n}.

For such
n
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{1+\varepsilon}}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq(5\sqrt{2})^{1+\varepsilon}(\sqrt{2})^{2+2\varepsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(|a_{j}|-1)^{2+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle<200\left(\sum_{|a|\geq\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{(|a|-1)^{2+2\varepsilon}}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{|a|\geq\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{(|a|-1)^{2+\varepsilon}}\right)^{n}.

Therefore, if

B_{\varepsilon}\colon=\left(\sum_{|a|\geq\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{(|a|-1)^{2+\varepsilon}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}},

we have
g_{n}(1+\varepsilon;\mathscr{D},B_{\varepsilon})<1
and
s_{n,B_{\varepsilon}}\leq 1+\varepsilon
. Taking
n\to\infty
, we conclude
s_{B_{\varepsilon}}\leq 1+\varepsilon
. Finally, since
B\mapsto s_{n,B}
is decreasing for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
B\to s_{B}
is also decreasing, so
s_{B}\to 1
when
B\to\infty
.

∎

Next, we show that
s_{B}
,
B>1
, is precisely the number defined in (
2
). In other words, in the definition of
g_{n}
, we can replace
5\sqrt{2}
with
1
and
\|\,\cdot\,\|
with
|\,\cdot\,|
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
G_{n}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

\forall\rho\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\qquad G_{n}(\rho;B)\colon=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\frac{1}{\left(B^{n}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}\right)^{\rho}}.

Define

S_{n,B}\colon=\inf\left\{\rho>0:G_{n}(\rho;B)\leq 1\right\}.

Corollary 8.13
.

We have

\lim_{n\to\infty}S_{n,B}=s_{B}.

Proof.

Note that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, we have

\frac{1}{B^{n}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}\leq\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}},

so
G_{n}(\rho;B)\leq g_{n}(\rho;B)
for all
\rho>0
, thus,
S_{n,B}\leq s_{n,B}
. Reversely, for any
\delta>0
, every large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
satisfy

\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{(B+\delta)^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{B^{n}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}.

Therefore,
g_{n}(\rho;B+\delta)\leq G_{n}(\rho;B)
, so
s_{n,B+\delta}\leq S_{n,B}
. By Lemma
8.7
, we obtain

s_{B+\delta}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}S_{n,B}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}S_{n,B}\leq s_{B}.

To finalize the proof, let
\delta\to 0
and apply Lemma
8.12
.
ii
.
∎

8.3.
Upper bound of Hausdorff dimension

In this subsection,
B>1
will remain fixed.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, define

J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\Cl\left(\{z\in\mathfrak{F}\colon a_{k}(z)=a_{k}\text{ for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\text{ and }\|a_{n+1}(z)\|\geq B^{n+1}\}\right).

Lemma 8.14
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
,

|J_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{2\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}+1)}{B^{n+1}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}},

where

\lambda_{1}\colon=\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-1}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.20
.
∎

Recall that

F(B)\colon=\{z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}\|\geq B^{n}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Proposition 8.15
.

We have

F(B)\subseteq\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{n\geq m}\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}J_{n}(\mathbf{b}).

Proof.

Take
z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in F(B)
. The result is obvious when
\mathbf{a}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathsf{R}
, so we assume that
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{Ir}
. Theorem
7.1
ensures that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is some
\mathbf{b}=(b_{n,1},\ldots,b_{n,n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that
z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{b})
and
\|b_{n,j}\|=\|a_{j}\|
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Hence, if
m\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
\|a_{m}\|\geq B^{m}
, then
\|b_{n,m}\|\geq B^{m}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq m}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
: Upper Bound.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. By Lemma
8.7
, we may pick
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{1}}
large enough so that any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N}
verifies
s_{n,B}<s_{B}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, which gives
s_{n,B}+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}<s_{B}+\varepsilon
and thus

g_{n}(s_{B}+\varepsilon;\mathscr{D},B)<1.

Put
\lambda_{2}\colon=2\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}+1)
. The
(s_{b}+2\varepsilon)
-Hausdorff measure of
F(B)
, denoted by
\mathcal{H}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}(F(B))
, is bounded above as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\left(F(B)\right)\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}|J_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{2}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{1}{B^{n+1}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{2}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}}{(5\sqrt{2}B)^{s_{B}+\varepsilon}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{B}+\varepsilon}\frac{1}{(B^{n+1}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2})^{\varepsilon}}

(by Lemma
8.14
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{2}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}}{(5\sqrt{2}B)^{s_{B}+\varepsilon}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\left(\frac{2\psi}{B^{n+1}\psi^{n}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{n}\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{B}+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\varepsilon}\psi^{\varepsilon}\lambda_{2}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}}{B^{\varepsilon}(5\sqrt{2}B)^{s_{B}+\varepsilon}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\frac{1}{(B^{\varepsilon}\psi^{\varepsilon})^{n}}g_{n}(s_{B}+\varepsilon;\mathscr{D},B)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{\varepsilon}\psi^{\varepsilon}\lambda_{2}^{s_{B}+2\varepsilon}}{B^{\varepsilon}(5\sqrt{2}B)^{s_{B}+\varepsilon}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\frac{1}{(B^{\varepsilon}\psi^{\varepsilon})^{n}}

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore,
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F(B))\leq s_{B}
.
∎

8.4.
Lower bound of Hausdorff dimension

In this subsection,
B>1
will remain fixed.
To prove the lower bound, we construct a family of Cantor subsets
\{F_{M}(B):M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 4}\}
such that
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F_{M}(B))\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F(B))
for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
.
Later, we estimate their Hausdorff dimension using the Mass Distribution Principle (see, for example,
[
18
, Theorem 4.2]
for a proof).

Lemma 8.16

(Mass Distribution Principle)
.

Let
F\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be non-empty and let
\mu
be a finite measure such that
\mu(F)>0
. If there are constants
c>0
,
r_{0}>0
and
t\geq 0
such that for all
z\in F
and all
r\in(0,r_{0})
we have
\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)\leq cr^{t}
, then
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F)\geq t
.

8.4.1.
Construction of Cantor subsets

Take
M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 4}
. Let
(n_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of natural numbers such that

(50)

\displaystyle B^{n_{1}-1}

\displaystyle>11,

(51)

\displaystyle n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n_{k+1}}{k+1}\,\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N}.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
D_{n}
be the set of words
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that every
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
satisfies:

i.

If
j=n_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+2\leq\|a_{n_{k}}\|\leq 2\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+1
;

ii.

If
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{n_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
, then
\|a_{j}\|\leq M
.

We put
D_{0}\colon=\varnothing
and
D\colon=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}D_{n}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathsf{R}(n)
, write

A_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathscr{D}\\
\mathbf{a}b\in D_{n+1}\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}b).

We refer to the sets
A_{n}(\mathbf{a})
as
fundamental sets
of level
n
. Define

F_{M}(B)\colon=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}}A_{n}(\mathbf{a}).

Observe that each union in the definition of
F_{M}(B)
is finite.

Lemma 8.17
.

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F_{M}(B))\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F(B))
.

Proof.

The countable stability of the Hausdorff dimension and its invariance under bi-Lipschtiz maps give
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F_{M}(B))=\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F_{M}(B)\cap\mathcal{C}_{1}(a))
for all
a\in\mathscr{D}
. Fix any
a
such that
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a)\subseteq\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}
; for instance,
a=3+3i
. Let us show that

F_{M}(B)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a)\subseteq F(B).

Take any
\xi=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in F_{M}(B)
. Let
(\mathbf{c}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence in
\mathscr{D}
such that
\mathbf{c}_{n}\in D_{n}
and
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\mathbf{c}_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Pick any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{c}_{n_{k}})
, then
\|a_{n_{k}}\|\geq\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+2>B^{n_{k}}
. Assume that
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{c}_{n_{k}})\setminus\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{c}_{n_{k}})
and write
\mathbf{c}_{n_{k}}=(c_{1,n_{k}},\ldots,c_{n_{k},n_{k}})
. If
\xi
is regular, then
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{c}_{n_{k}})\cap\mathcal{C}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}})
and, by Corollary
6.15
,

\|a_{n_{k}}\|\geq\|c^{n_{k}}_{n_{k}}\|-1\geq\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+1>B^{n_{k}}.

If
\xi
is irregular, there is some
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n_{k}})\in\mathsf{R}(n)
such that
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{b})
and
\|b_{j}\|=\|a_{j}\|
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n_{k}\}
. Therefore,
\xi\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{c}_{n_{k}})\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{b})
and

\|a_{n_{k}}\|=\|b_{n_{k}}\|\geq\|c^{n_{k}}_{n_{k}}\|-1\geq\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+1>B^{n_{k}}.

Since
k
was arbitrary, we conclude that
\xi\in F(B)
.
∎

8.4.2.
Probability measure on
F_{M}(B)

Let
(m_{j})_{j\geq 1}
be the sequence given by

m_{1}\colon=n_{1}-1\quad\text{ and }\quad m_{j}\colon=n_{j}-n_{j-1}-1\text{ for all }j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}.

First, take
n\in\{1,\ldots,n_{1}\}
.

i.

If
n=n_{1}-1
, for each
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{1}-1}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n_{1}-1}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{1}}\|q_{m_{1}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{1},B}(M)}.

ii.

If
n=n_{1}
, for each
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{1}})\in D_{n_{1}}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n_{1}}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\frac{\mu\left(A_{n_{1}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{1}-1})\right)}{\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{1}-1},d)\in D_{n_{1}}\}}.

iii.

If
n\in\{1,\ldots,n_{1}-2\}
, for each
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n_{1}-n-1)\\
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in D_{n_{1}-1}\end{subarray}}\mu\left(A_{n_{1}-1}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})\right).

We now consider
n\in\{n_{1}+1,\ldots,n_{2}-1\}
.

i.

If
n=n_{2}-1
, for each
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{2}-1})\in D_{n_{2}-1}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n_{2}-1}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\mu(A_{n_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{1}}))\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{2}}\|q_{m_{2}}(a_{n_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{n_{2}-1})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{2},B}(M)}.

ii.

If
n\in\{n_{1}+1,\ldots,n_{2}-2\}
, for each
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in D_{n}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n_{2}-1-n)\\
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in D_{n_{2}-1}\end{subarray}}\mu\left(A_{n_{2}-1}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})\right).

Assume that, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, we have already defined
\mu(A_{j}(\mathbf{a}))
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n_{k}-1\}
and
\mathbf{a}\in D_{j}
. Take
n\in\{n_{k},\ldots,n_{k+1}-1\}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in D_{n}
.

i.

If
n=n_{k}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\frac{\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)}{\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{k}}\}}.

ii.

If
n=n_{k+1}-1
, put

\mu\left(A_{n_{k+1}-1}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\mu(A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}}))\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k+1}}\|q_{m_{k+1}}(a_{n_{k}+1},\ldots,a_{n_{k+1}-1})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k+1},B}(M)}.

iii.

If
n\in\{n_{k}+1,\ldots,n_{k+1}-2\}
, put

\mu\left(A_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\colon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{R}(n_{k+1}-1-n)\\
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\in D_{n_{k+1}-1}\end{subarray}}\mu\left(A_{n_{k+1}-1}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b})\right).

Finally, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in D_{n}
with
\mathbf{a}\neq\mathbf{b}
we define
\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a})\cap A_{n}(\mathbf{b}))=0
.

Let us explain why
\mu
is well defined. First, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the infimum defining
s_{m,B}(M)
is actually a minimum, because the involved sum is finite. As a consequence, we have

\mu\left(\bigcup_{\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{1}-1}}A_{n_{1}-1}(\mathbf{a})\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{1}-1}}\mu\left(A_{n_{1}-1}(\mathbf{a})\right)=1.

Second, given
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{1}-1}
, we defined
\mu
on the sets
\{A_{n_{1}}(\mathbf{a}d):\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{1}}\}
by uniformly distributing the mass
\mu(A_{n_{1}-1}(\mathbf{a}))
among them, so

\mu(A_{n_{1}-1}(\mathbf{a}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in\mathscr{D}\\
\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mu(A_{n_{1}}(\mathbf{a}d)).

The definition of
\mu
ensures that the previous equation holds if we replace
n_{1}
with any
n\in\{1,\ldots,n_{1}-1\}
. Moreover, it still holds for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, by the Daniell-Kolmogorov consistency theorem (see, for example,
[
40
, Corollary 8.22]
), the function
\mu
extends to a unique Borel probability measure supported on
F_{M}(B)
.

8.4.3.
Bounding
\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}))

In this section, we give an upper estimate of the
\mu
measure of the sets
A_{n}(\mathbf{a})
in terms of
|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|
(Lemma
8.21
). The proof relies on estimates of a geometric (Propositions
8.18
,
8.19
) and arithmetic nature (Proposition
8.20
).

Proposition 8.18
.

If
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{k}-1}
, then

2\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}\leq\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{k}}\}\leq 14\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}.

Proof.

Pick any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and write
B_{1}\colon=\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+2
and
B_{2}\colon=2\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+1
. For the upper bound, we have

\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{k}}\}\leq\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:B_{1}\leq\|d\|\leq B_{2}\}

and, since
B_{1}\geq 3
,

\displaystyle(2B_{2}+1)^{2}-(2B_{1}-1)^{2}

\displaystyle=4(B_{2}-B_{1})(B_{2}+B_{1}+1)

\displaystyle=4(\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor-1)(3\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+3)

\displaystyle\leq 14\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}.

For the lower bound, note that
\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{k}}\}
attains its minimum when
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)\pmod{\mathsf{Rot}}
. Then,

\displaystyle(B_{2}+1)^{2}-(B_{1}+1)^{2}

\displaystyle=(B_{2}-B_{1})(B_{2}+B_{1}+2)

\displaystyle=(\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor-1)(3\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor+4)

\displaystyle\geq 2\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}.

∎

Define
\kappa_{5}\colon=\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
, with
\kappa_{1}
as in Proposition
5.15
.
i
, and
\kappa_{6}\colon=\frac{20}{3}
.

Proposition 8.19
.

The following estimates hold for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

i.

If
n=n_{k}-1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then every
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{k}-1}
satisfies

\frac{\kappa_{5}}{\|q_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor}\leq|A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{\kappa_{6}}{\|q_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor}.

ii.

If
n=n_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then every
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{k}}
satisfies

\frac{\kappa_{5}}{\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\leq|A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{\kappa_{6}}{\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}.

iii.

If
n_{k-1}<n<n_{k}-1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then every
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}
satisfies

\frac{\kappa_{5}}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}\leq|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{\kappa_{6}}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2}}.

Proof.

The estimates are a direct consequence of Proposition
5.15
. We point out that when
n\in\mathbb{N}
falls in cases
ii
and
iii
, we have
|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})|=|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|
for any
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}
.
∎

The next proposition contains two important bounds. The first one can be obtained inductively. The second one is a consequence of Proposition
5.15
.
ii
. We omit the details.

Proposition 8.20
.

i.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)\cap\mathscr{D}(M)
, we have
\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|\leq(2M+1)^{n}
.

ii.

Let
(t_{i})_{j\geq 1}
be a sequence of natural numbers and define
(T_{j})_{j\geq 1}
by
T_{j}\colon=t_{1}+\ldots+t_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{T_{n}})\in\Omega(T_{n})
,

6^{n-1}\|q_{T_{n}}(\mathbf{a})\|\leq\|q_{t_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{t_{1}})\|\cdots\|q_{t_{n}}(a_{T_{n-1}+1},\ldots,a_{T_{n}})\|.

Lemma 8.21
.

For each
0<t<s_{B}(M)
, there are
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
c_{0}>0
such that every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq k_{0}}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq n_{k}}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}
satisfy

\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}))\leq c_{0}|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}.

Proof.

Pick any
0<t<s_{B}(M)
. Choose a number
\gamma
such that
1<\gamma<\psi
. Define
\varepsilon
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}
by

\varepsilon\colon=\frac{s_{B}(M)-t}{4},\quad\tilde{\varepsilon}\colon=\frac{\log\gamma}{\log(B)+2\log(2M+1)}\,\varepsilon.

Let
N_{3}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{2}}
be such that

\displaystyle|s_{n,B}(M)-s_{l,B}(M)|<\tilde{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\;\text{ for all }n,l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{3}},

\displaystyle t+2\varepsilon<s_{n,B}(M)

\displaystyle\;\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{3}}.

Without any reference, we will use that

t<t+\varepsilon<t+2\varepsilon<s_{n,B}(M)\leq 2\quad\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq N_{3}}.

Define
\kappa_{7}\colon=81000
and let
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq k_{0}+1}
has the following properties:

(52)

m_{k}\geq N_{3},

(53)

\frac{n_{k}}{m_{k}}\leq 1+\frac{2\varepsilon}{t+2\varepsilon},

(54)

\frac{\log\kappa_{7}}{2k\varepsilon\log B}+\frac{\log(B^{2N_{3}}(2M+1)^{4N_{3}})+\log(6^{8}\cdot 50)-\log\kappa_{5}^{2}-\log\kappa(\hat{s})}{2k\varepsilon\log B}\leq\frac{1}{k-1}\sum_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}n_{j}.

The inequality (
54
) is actually attained by every large
k
because of (
51
). For notational convenience, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, we write
q_{m_{j}}
rather than
q_{m_{j}}(a_{n_{j-1}+1},\ldots,a_{n_{j}-1})
. Lastly, for

c_{0}\colon=(2M+1)^{4n_{k_{0}}}B^{4(n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k_{0}})},

Proposition
8.20
.
i
guarantees

(55)

\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{j},B}(M)}\leq 1\leq c_{0}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+\varepsilon}.

Although all of these parameters depend on
t
, the order we used to define them is relevant. We have the following dependencies:
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(t)
,
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\tilde{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon)
,
N_{3}=N_{3}(\varepsilon,\tilde{\varepsilon})
,
k_{0}=k_{0}(\varepsilon,N_{3})
, and
c_{0}=c_{0}(k_{0})
.

Take
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n\geq n_{k_{0}+1}
and
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}
. We now estimate
\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}))
.

8.4.4.
Case
n=n_{k}

For any
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}})\in D_{n_{k}}
, the definition of
\mu
and Proposition
8.18
yield

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{j},B}(M)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{j},B}(M)}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{j},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}c_{0}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+\varepsilon}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}\qquad\text{(by \eqref{EQ:COND-ON-nk-01} and \eqref{Eq:CondOn_c0})}

(56)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{50^{k_{0}}}{2^{k}}c_{0}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{(B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}.

Let us focus on the last product:

\displaystyle\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{2^{2}}{B^{2n_{j}}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}4\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}+m_{j}(t+2\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}4\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{(n_{j}+m_{j})(t+2\varepsilon)}}
