\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{200}{\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2(t+2\varepsilon)}}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{(n_{j}+m_{j})(t+2\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{200}{\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2(t+2\varepsilon)}}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}(t+\varepsilon)}}\qquad\text{(by \eqref{EQ:COND-ON-nk-02})}

\displaystyle\leq 200^{k-k_{0}}\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2(t+\varepsilon)}}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}(t+\varepsilon)}}

(57)

\displaystyle=200^{k-k_{0}}\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{(B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}\;\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}\varepsilon}}.

We substitute (
57
) in (
56
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle\leq 100^{k}\frac{c_{0}}{4^{k_{0}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{1}{(B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{(B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq 100^{k}\frac{c_{0}}{4^{k_{0}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{(B^{2n_{j}}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}\varepsilon}}

(58)

\displaystyle\leq 100^{k}c_{0}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{(\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}.

Since for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\|a_{n_{j}}\|\leq 2\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor+1
, we get
\|a_{n_{j}}\|\leq 3\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor
and

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{(\lfloor B^{n_{j}}\rfloor^{2}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2})^{t+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\frac{9}{\|a_{n_{j}}\|^{2}\|q_{m_{j}}\|^{2}}\right)^{t+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq 9^{k(t+\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{6^{2k-1}\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\|}\right)^{2(t+\varepsilon)}

(by Proposition
8.20
.
ii
)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{9^{t+\varepsilon}}{6^{4t}}\right)^{k}6^{2t}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq 81^{k}6^{4}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}.

Hence, plugging the previous inequality into (
58
), we have

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}8100^{k}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}8100^{k}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\kappa_{5}^{t}}|A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}

(by Proposition
8.19
)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}8100^{k}6^{4}\frac{1}{\kappa_{5}^{t}}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}|A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}

\displaystyle=c_{0}6^{4}\frac{\kappa_{7}^{k}}{\kappa_{5}^{2}}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}|A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}|A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}.

The last inequality follows from (
54
). This finishes the proof for the case
n=n_{k}
. We state the next corollary for reference.

Corollary 8.22
.

If
r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq k_{0}}
and
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{r}}
, then

\mu(A_{n_{r}}(\mathbf{a}))\leq c_{0}6^{4}\kappa_{7}^{r}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{r}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{r}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}.

8.4.5.
Case
n=n_{k}-1

Take
k\in\mathbb{N}_{k_{0}+1}
,
\mathbf{a}\in D_{n_{k}-1}
and let
b\in\mathscr{D}
be such that
\mathbf{a}b\in D_{n_{k}}
. Then, using the definition of
\mu
and Corollary
8.22
,

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle=\mu(A_{n_{k-1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k-1}}))\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{\|q_{m_{k}}\|^{2}B^{m_{k}}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k-1}}\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{\|q_{m_{k}}\|^{2}B^{m_{k}}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k-1}}\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{\|q_{m_{k}}\|^{2}B^{m_{k}}}\right)^{t+2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{50}{(\|q_{n_{k-1}}\|\|q_{m_{k}}\|)^{2(t+\varepsilon)}}\frac{1}{B^{m_{k}(t+2\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}50\cdot 6^{8}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}-1}\|^{2(t+\varepsilon)}}\frac{1}{B^{tn_{k}}}

(by Proposition
8.20
.
ii
)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}50\cdot 6^{8}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{(B^{n_{k}}\|q_{n_{k}-1}\|^{2})^{t}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}50\cdot 6^{8}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{(\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor\|q_{n_{k}-1}\|^{2})^{t}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}50\cdot 6^{8}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\kappa_{5}^{t}}|A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}

(by Proposition
8.19
)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}|A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}.

(by (
54
))

8.4.6.
Case
n_{k-1}<n<n_{k}-1

Put

l^{\prime}\colon=n_{k}-n-1\text{ and }l\colon=n-n_{k-1}.

Letting
\mathbf{b}
run along the set
\{\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(l^{\prime}):\mathbf{a}\mathbf{c}\in D_{n_{k}-1}\}
, applying Corollary
8.22
, and writing
\mathbf{a}[\alpha,\beta]=(a_{\alpha},a_{\alpha+1},\ldots,a_{\beta})
for natural numbers
\alpha<\beta
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}))=\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\mu(A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\mu(A_{n_{k-1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k-1}}))\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{m_{k}}(\mathbf{a}[n_{k-1}+1,n]\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k-1}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{m_{k}}(\mathbf{a}[n_{k-1}+1,n]\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k-1}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\left(\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{l}(\mathbf{a}[n_{k-1}+1,n])\|^{2}\|q_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{(5\sqrt{2})^{s_{m_{k},B(M)}}}{\|q_{n_{k-1}}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}\|q_{l}(\mathbf{a}[n_{k-1}+1,n])\|^{2s_{m_{k},B}(M)}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k-1}}(\mathbf{a})\|\,\|q_{l}(\mathbf{a}[n_{k-1}+1,n])\|}\right)^{2t+2\varepsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{4}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{6}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|}\right)^{2t+2\varepsilon}\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{b})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

(59)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}g_{l^{\prime}}(s_{m_{k},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B).

Since
l+l^{\prime}=m_{k}
, in view of (
46
), we have

\kappa(\hat{s})g_{l^{\prime}}(s_{m_{k},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)g_{l}(s_{m_{k},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)\leq g_{m_{k}}(s_{m_{j},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)=1,

so

g_{l^{\prime}}(s_{m_{k},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)\leq\frac{1}{\kappa(\hat{s})g_{l}(s_{m_{k},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)}.

We, thus, need a lower bound of
g_{l}(s_{m_{j},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)
. We obtain it by considering two cases:
l\leq N_{3}
and
l>N_{3}
. If
l\leq N_{3}
, then

\displaystyle g_{l}(s_{m_{k},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{D}(M)^{l}\cap\mathsf{R}(l)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{l}\|q_{l}(\mathbf{c})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{B^{N_{3}s_{m_{k},B}(M)}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{D}(M)^{l}\cap\mathsf{R}(l)}\frac{1}{\|q_{l}(\mathbf{c})\|^{2s_{m_{k},B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{B^{2N_{3}}(2M+1)^{4N_{3}}},

so, by (
59
) and Proposition
8.20
,

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}}{\kappa(\hat{s})}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}B^{2N_{3}}(2M+1)^{4N_{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}}{\kappa(\hat{s})}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}B^{2N_{3}}(2M+1)^{4N_{3}}\frac{|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t+\varepsilon}}{\kappa_{5}^{t+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}\kappa_{5}^{2}}{\kappa(\hat{s})}B^{2N_{3}}(2M+1)^{4N_{3}}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}.

Now assume that
l>N_{3}
. The definitions of
N_{3}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
yield

\displaystyle g_{l}(s_{m_{j},B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{D}(M)^{l}\cap\mathsf{R}(l)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{l}\|q_{l}(\mathbf{c})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{D}(M)^{l}\cap\mathsf{R}(l)}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{l}\|q_{l}(\mathbf{c})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{l,B}(M)+\tilde{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{D}(M)^{l}\cap\mathsf{R}(l)}\frac{1}{(B^{l}(2M+1)^{2l})^{\tilde{\varepsilon}}}\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{l}\|q_{l}(\mathbf{c})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{l,B}(M)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(B^{l}(2M+1)^{2l})^{\tilde{\varepsilon}}}g_{l}(s_{l,B}(M);\mathscr{D}(M),B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(B^{l}(2M+1)^{2l})^{\tilde{\varepsilon}}}\geq\frac{1}{\gamma^{l\varepsilon}}\geq\frac{1}{\gamma^{n\varepsilon}}.

The previous inequality and (
59
) imply that

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}}{\hat{\kappa}(s)}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{\gamma^{n\varepsilon}}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}}{\hat{\kappa}(s)}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{\gamma^{n\varepsilon}}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})\|^{2t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}50\kappa_{7}^{k-1}6^{8}\psi^{\varepsilon}}{\kappa(\hat{s})\kappa_{5}^{t}}\left(\prod_{j=k_{0}+1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{B^{2n_{j}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{\gamma^{n\varepsilon}}{\psi^{n\varepsilon}}|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}.

∎

8.4.7.
Estimate of
\mu(\mathbb{D}(z,r))

In the context of regular continued fractions, the measure of the balls is bounded by estimating the length of the gaps between two different fundamental sets (see
[
55
, Section 3.2]
). The strategy must be modified for Hcfs. Let us illustrate why, with a simple example. When
M=5
and
n_{1}\geq 3
, both
a=3+3i
and
2+3i
belong to
D_{1}
but
A_{1}(a)\cap A_{1}(b)\neq\varnothing
(see Figure
12
). We may thus lose any hope of obtaining a non-trivial lower bound of the distance between any two fundamental sets of the same level. This is when our discussion on the geometry of Hcfs pays off.

Figure 12.
The sets
\iota[A_{1}(2+3i)]
and
\iota[A_{1}(3+3i)]
.

Let
\tilde{\rho}
and
\rho
be as in Theorems
6.3
and
6.16
, respectively. Set
\rho^{\prime}\colon=\min\{\tilde{\rho},\rho\}
and let
\kappa_{3}
be as in Corollary
6.14
. For each
\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\prefi(\mathbf{a};n)\in D_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\tilde{G}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{\kappa_{3}}|A_{n}(\mathbf{a})|.

Let
r_{0}
be

r_{0}\colon=\min\left\{\tilde{G}_{n}(\mathbf{a}):n\in\{1,\ldots,n_{k_{0}}\},\,\mathbf{a}\in D_{n}\right\}.

Lemma 8.23
.

There is a constant
\nu=\nu(M,B,t)>0
such that for all
z\in F_{M}(B)
and every
r\in(0,r_{0})
we have
\mu(\mathbb{D}(z;r))\leq\nu r^{t}
.

Proof.

Take a real number
r
with
0<r<r_{0}
,
z\in F_{M}(B)
, and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
z\in A_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,j)\in D_{j}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

\tilde{G}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1})\leq r<\tilde{G}_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

We examine three different cases for
n
.

8.4.8.
Case
n=n_{k}-1

First, we further assume
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
,
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+2i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
. Then, the disc
\mathbb{D}(z;r)
intersects exactly one fundamental set of order
n
. We now consider two possibilities:

r\leq\kappa_{3}|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}})|\;\text{ or }\;\kappa_{3}|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}})|<r.

In the first option,
\mathbb{D}(z;r)
intersects at most six cylinders of level
n_{k}
, which are
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\prefi(\mathbf{a};n))
and five of its neighbors, so it intersects at most six fundamental sets of level
n_{k}
, hence

\mu(\mathbb{D}(z;r))\leq 6\kappa_{5}c_{0}|A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}<6\kappa_{5}c_{0}\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\tilde{\rho}}r^{t}.

Now assume that
\kappa_{3}|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}})|<r
. Define
\mathsf{N}\colon=\#\left\{\mathbf{d}\in D_{n_{k}}:\mathbb{D}(z;r)\cap A_{n_{k}}(\mathbf{d})\neq\varnothing\right\}
. Let us show that there is an absolute constant
\nu_{2}
such that

(60)

\mathsf{N}\leq 6^{4}2^{11}\pi\nu_{2}^{2}\|q_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{4}r^{2}.

First, note that there is an absolute constant
\nu_{1}
such that for all
c,d\in\mathscr{D}
satisfying
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},c)\in D_{n_{k}}
and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)\in D_{n_{k}}
we have

(61)

\frac{1}{\nu_{1}}\leq\frac{|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},c)|}{|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)|}\leq\nu_{1}.

Certainly, since
\frac{1}{3}\leq\frac{\|d\|}{\|c\|}\leq 3
,

\frac{|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},c)|}{|A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)|}=\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},c)|}{|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)|}\asymp\frac{|q_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)|^{2}}{|q_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},c)|^{2}}\asymp\frac{\|d\|^{2}}{\|c\|^{2}}\asymp 1.

Then, for
\nu_{2}\colon=1+\frac{\nu_{1}}{\kappa_{3}}
, we have that
A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)\cap\mathbb{D}(z;r)\neq\varnothing
implies that
A_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)\subseteq\mathbb{D}(z;\nu_{2}r)
. Therefore, in view of the estimate

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\left(\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2^{2}\|q_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1},d)\|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}6^{-4}}{2^{2}\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\|d\|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}6^{-4}}{2^{2}\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}2^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{4}\left(1+\frac{1}{2\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor}\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{6^{4}2^{11}}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{4}},

we get (
60
). We, thus, conclude that

\displaystyle\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)}{\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{a}d\in D_{n_{k}}\}}\mathsf{N}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6^{4}2^{11}\pi\nu_{2}^{2}}{2\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}}\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{4}r^{2}

(62)

\displaystyle=6^{4}2^{10}\pi\nu_{2}^{2}\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}r^{2}.

Moreover, since
A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{a})
is the single fundamental set of level
n_{k}-1
intersecting
\mathbb{D}(z;r)
, we have that

(63)

\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)=\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\cap A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)\leq\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right).

Combine inequalities (
62
) and (
63
) and recall that
t<2
, to see that for some constants
\nu_{3},\nu_{3}^{\prime}>0
we have

\displaystyle\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)\min\left\{1,6^{4}2^{10}\pi\nu_{2}^{2}\|q_{n_{k}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}r^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)1^{1-\tfrac{t}{2}}\left(6^{4}2^{10}\pi\nu_{2}^{2}\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{4}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{2}r^{2}\right)^{\frac{t}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{3}^{\prime}\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)\|q_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\|^{2t}\lfloor B^{n_{k}}\rfloor^{t}r^{t}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\nu_{3}r^{t}.

Now, assume that either
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(2)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
or
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\equiv\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)\mod\mathsf{Rot}
. By the Second Geometric Construction (Theorem
6.16
), the disc
\mathbb{D}(z;r)
intersects at most two fundamental sets of level
n_{k}-1
and they are subsets of neighboring
n_{k}-1
cylinders, say
A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})
and
A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b})
. Hence,

\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)=\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\cap A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-1})\right)+\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\cap A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b})\right).

Propping on the above argument we may then assert that, for some constant
\nu_{4}=\nu_{4}(M,B,t)>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{3}c_{0}|A_{n_{k}-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k_{n}-1})|^{t}+\nu_{3}c_{0}|A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b})|^{t}

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\nu_{3}(1+\kappa_{3})r^{t}.

8.4.9.
Case
n=n_{k}-2

First, note that for any
\mathbf{b}\in D_{n_{k}-2}
and
c,d\in\mathscr{D}
such that
\mathbf{b}c,\mathbf{b}d\in D_{n_{k}-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b}c)\right)}{\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b}d)\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\|q_{m_{k}}(\mathbf{b}d)\|^{2}}{\|q_{m_{k}}(\mathbf{b}c)\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1800\frac{\|d\|^{2}}{\|c\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1800M^{2}\right)^{s_{m_{k},B}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq 1800^{2}M^{4}.

(Proposition
8.9
)

As a consequence, using
\#\{c\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{b}c\in D_{n_{k}-1}\}\leq\#\{d\in\mathscr{D}:\|d\|\leq M\}\leq(2M+1)^{2}
, we have

\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-2}(\mathbf{b})\right)\leq 8\kappa_{5}1800^{2}M^{4}(2M+1)^{2}c_{0}\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b}c)\right).

Finally, since
\mathbb{D}(z;r)
intersects at most six fundamental sets of level
n_{k}-2
, one of them being
A_{n_{k}-2}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-2})
and the others are contained in the neighboring cylinders of
\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-2}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n_{k}-2})
, we have

\mu\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)\leq 48\kappa_{5}1800^{2}M^{4}(2M+1)^{2}c_{0}r^{t}.

8.4.10.
Case
n_{k-1}\leq n\leq n_{k}-3

Consider any
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in D_{n}
and any
c,d\in\mathscr{D}
such that
\mathbf{b}c
,
\mathbf{c}d\in D_{n+1}
. We show the existence of some constant
\nu_{5}>0
for which

(64)

\frac{\mu\left(A_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}c)\right)}{\mu\left(A_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}d)\right)}\leq 4\cdot 3600^{2\nu_{5}}M^{2\nu_{5}}.

Once this is achieved, the argument in the case
n=n_{k}-2
can be applied.

First, observe that we may express
\mu(A_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}c))
as follows (we sum over all the words
\mathbf{c}\in\mathsf{R}(n_{k}-n-2)
such that
\mathbf{b}\,c\,\mathbf{c}\in D_{n_{k}-1})
:

\displaystyle\mu\left(A_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}c)\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}}\mu\left(A_{n_{k}-1}(\mathbf{b}c\mathbf{c})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}}\mu\left(A_{n_{k-1}}(\prefi(\mathbf{b};n_{k-1}))\right)\left(\frac{5\sqrt{2}}{B^{m_{k}}\|q_{m_{k}}(b_{n_{k-1}},\ldots,b_{n},c,\mathbf{c})\|^{2}}\right)^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu\left(A_{n_{k-1}}(\prefi(\mathbf{b};n_{k-1}))\right)(5\sqrt{2})^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}{B^{m_{k}s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}}\frac{1}{\|q_{m_{k}}(b_{n_{k-1}},\ldots,b_{n},c,\mathbf{c})\|^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}.

Next, we estimate the sum in the last expression. As an upper estimate, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|q_{m_{k}}(b_{n_{k-1}},\ldots,b_{n},c,\mathbf{c})\|^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{50}{\|q_{n-n_{k-1}+1}(b_{n_{k}},\ldots,b_{n})\|\|c\|}\right)^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}\sum_{\mathbf{c}}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{c})\|^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{50}{\kappa_{5}\|q_{n-n_{k}+1}(b_{n_{k}},\ldots,b_{n})\|\|c\|}\right)^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}\sum_{\mathbf{c}}|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{c})|^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}.

In the next lower bound, the sums run along those words
\mathbf{d}\in\mathsf{R}(n_{k}-n-2)
such that
\mathbf{b}\,d\,\mathbf{d}\in D_{n_{k}-1}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{d}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|q_{m_{k}}(b_{n_{k-1}},\ldots,b_{n},d,\mathbf{d})\|^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{36\|q_{n-n_{k-1}+1}(b_{n_{k}},\ldots,b_{n})\|\|d\|}\right)^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}\sum_{\mathbf{d}}\frac{1}{\|q_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{d})\|^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{72\|q_{n-n_{k}+1}(b_{n_{k}},\ldots b_{n})\|\|d\|}\right)^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}\sum_{\mathbf{d}}|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{d})|^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}.

Hence, if
\nu_{5}\colon=\nu_{5}(M,B)\colon=\sup\{s_{n,B}(M):n\in\mathbb{N}\}
(see Proposition
8.9
), then

\displaystyle\frac{\mu\left(A_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}c)\right)}{\mu\left(A_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}d)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(3600\frac{\|d\|}{\|c\|}\right)^{2s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{c}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{c})\right|^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}{\sum_{\mathbf{d}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{d})\right|^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\leq 3600^{2\nu}M^{2\nu}\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{c}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{c})\right|^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}{\sum_{\mathbf{d}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{n_{k}-n-2}(\mathbf{d})\right|^{s_{n_{k}-n-2,B}(M)}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot 3600^{2\nu_{5}}M^{2\nu_{5}}.

For the last inequality, we considered
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}c)=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
in the numerator and
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{b}c)=\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}(1+i)
in the denominator. This shows (
64
). ∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
: Lower bound.

For any
M\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 4}
, the Mass Distribution Principle applied to
F_{M}(B)
implies that
s_{B}(M)\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F_{M}(B))
. Therefore, by lemmas
8.17
and
8.9
,

s_{B}=\lim_{M\to\infty}s_{B}(M)\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(F(B)).

∎

The proof of Theorem
1.3
is flexible enough to assume that the terms of the sequence
(n_{j})_{j\geq 1}
lie in a prescribed infinite subset of
\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathscr{L}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be infinite and, for each
B>1
, define

E(B;\mathscr{L})\colon=\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}\|\geq B^{n}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathscr{L}\right\}.

Corollary 8.24
.

If
\mathscr{L}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
is infinite and
B>1
, then
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E(B;\mathscr{L})\right)=s_{B}
.

9.
A complex Łuczak Theorem

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.5
. First, we observe that we can replace
|\,\cdot\,|
with
\|\,\cdot\,\|
. For
b,c>1
, define the sets

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)

\displaystyle\colon=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq\|a_{n}(\xi)\|\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\},

\displaystyle\Xi(b,c)

\displaystyle\colon=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq\|a_{n}(\xi)\|\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

On the one hand, since
\|z\|\leq|z|
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)\subseteq\{\xi\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq|a_{n}(\xi)|\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

On the other hand, if
\varepsilon>0
is such that
c-\varepsilon>1
, then every large
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

(c-\varepsilon)^{b^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}c^{b^{n}}

and, since
|z|\leq\sqrt{2}\|z\|
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we conclude

\{\xi\in\mathfrak{F}:c^{b^{n}}\leq|a_{n}(\xi)|\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\}\subseteq\Xi(b,c-\varepsilon).

Hence, Theorem
1.5
is equivalent to Theorem
9.1
below.

Theorem 9.1
.

If
b,c>1
, then

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\Xi(b,c)\right)=\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)\right)=\frac{2}{b+1}.

For the rest of this section, the numbers
b,c>1
will remain fixed. In view of
\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)\subseteq\Xi(b,c)
, we will obtain Theorem
9.1
from

(65)

\frac{2}{b+1}\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\Xi(b,c)\right)\leq\frac{2}{b+1}.

9.1.
Upper bound of Hausdorff dimension

This subsection is devoted to proving the upper bound in (
65
). Our argument is inspired in
[
52
]
.
A previous version of this paper (available on arXiv) contains a proof extending Łuczak’s original argument.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
s,d\in\mathbb{R}
be such that

\frac{1}{b}<s<1\;\text{ and }\;\frac{1}{s}<d<b.

Then, for every
z\in\Xi(b,c)
there are infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(66)

\|a_{n+1}(z)\|\geq\left(\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\right)^{sd-1}c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}.

Proof.

Take any
z\in\Xi(b,c)
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{>m}
such that

\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{m}(z)\|<c^{b^{k}d^{m-k}}\;\text{ and }\;c^{b^{k}}<\|a_{k}(z)\|.

Define the function
f_{k}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
by

f_{k}(N)=c^{b^{k}d^{N-k}}\quad\text{ for all }N\in\mathbb{N}.

Define

n=\max\{r\in\mathbb{N}:m\leq r\leq k-1,\;\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{r}(z)\|<f_{k}(r)\}.

Then,

\displaystyle\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\|a_{n+1}(z)\|

\displaystyle\geq f_{k}(n+1)

\displaystyle=f_{k}^{s}(n+1)f_{k}^{1-s}(n+1)

\displaystyle>f_{k}^{sd}(n)c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}

\displaystyle>\left(\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\right)^{sd}c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}.

Divide both sides by
\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|
and the desired result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.1
: Upper bound.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\Omega
, define the subset
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
of
\mathscr{D}
by

\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{a}):=\left\{b\in\mathscr{D}:\mathbf{a}b\in\Omega(\mathbf{a}b),\;\|b\|\geq\left(\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\right)^{sd-1}c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}\right\}

and the subset
K_{n}(\mathbf{a})
of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
by

K_{n}(\mathbf{a})\colon=\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{a})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}b)=Q_{n}\left(\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{a})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(b)\cap\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a});\mathbf{a}\right).

We may show inductively that
|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\geq\tau^{-n}\|a_{1}\|\cdots\|a_{n}\|
for
\tau=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}-1}
. Therefore, Proposition
4.2
.
i
and the definition of
K_{n}(\mathbf{a})
yield

\displaystyle|K_{n}(\mathbf{a})|

\displaystyle\asymp\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|\left|\bigcup_{b\in\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{a})}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(b)\cap\mathfrak{F}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|

\displaystyle\asymp\left|\mathcal{C}_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|\frac{1}{\left(\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\right)^{sd-1}c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{1}{|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}}\frac{1}{\left(\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\right)^{sd-1}c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{\tau^{n}}{\left(\|a_{1}(z)\|\cdots\|a_{n}(z)\|\right)^{sd+1}c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}}.

Let
F(b,c,s)
be the set for which (
66
) holds for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so
F(b,c,s)\supseteq\Xi(b,c)
. Then, if
t=\frac{2}{s(sd+1)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{t}\left(F(b,c,s)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)}\left|K_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|^{t}

\displaystyle\ll\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Omega(n)}\frac{\tau^{tn}}{\left(\|a_{1}\|\cdots\|a_{n}\|\right)^{\frac{2}{s}}c^{(1-s)td^{n+1}}}

\displaystyle\ll\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\frac{\tau^{tn}}{c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathscr{D}^{n}}\frac{1}{\left(\|a_{1}\|\cdots\|a_{n}\|\right)^{\frac{2}{s}}}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\geq N}\frac{1}{c^{(1-s)d^{n+1}}}\left(\tau^{t}\sum_{a\in\mathscr{D}}\frac{1}{\|a\|^{\frac{2}{s}}}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore,
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\Xi(b,c)\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}F(b,c,s)\leq t
and the upper bound in (
65
) follows by letting
s\to 1
and
d\to b
.
∎

9.2.
Lower bound of Hausdorff dimension

In this subsection, we obtain the lower bound in (
65
) by applying the Mass Distribution Principle on a Cantor subset
E
contained in
\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)
.

9.2.1.
Construction of
E

Let
\kappa_{1}
be as in Proposition
5.15
.
i
and take a natural number
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

M_{0}\geq\log_{b}\log_{c}\left(\frac{8\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}{\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right).

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the sets

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{n}

\displaystyle\colon=\left\{d\in\mathscr{D}:c^{b^{M_{0}+n+1}}\leq\|d\|\leq 2c^{b^{M_{0}+n+1}}\right\}\ \text{and}\ \Pre(n)\colon=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{D}_{j}.

Note that, by
M_{0}\geq\log_{b}\log_{c}3
and Proposition
5.9
, any sequence
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathscr{D}
such that
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\Pre(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
is full.

Next, we define the layers of compact sets determining
E
. Put

J_{0}\colon=\bigcup_{a_{1}\in\widetilde{D}_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a_{1})

and for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)
define

J_{n}(\mathbf{a}):=\bigcup_{b\in\widetilde{D}_{n+1}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}b).

We define
E
to be

E\colon=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)}J_{n}(\mathbf{a}).

We claim that
E\subseteq\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)
. Certainly, let
z\in E
be arbitrary.
Consider any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and take
\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)
,
b\in\tilde{D}_{n+1}
such that
z\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}b)
. By the proof of Proposition
5.9
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}b)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n}(\mathbf{a})
, so the first
n
partial quotients of
z
are precisely
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
. Hence,
z\in\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)
. The lower bound in (
65
) is, thus, a consequence of

(67)

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E)\geq\frac{2}{1+b}.

9.2.2.
A measure on
E

We define a probability measure
\tilde{\mu}
on
E
using a sequence of consistent measures. First, for every
a_{1}\in\widetilde{D}_{1}
, define

\tilde{\mu}_{1}(J_{1}(a_{1}))\colon=\frac{1}{\#\widetilde{D}_{1}}.

Take
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that we have defined
\tilde{\mu}_{n}(J_{n}(\mathbf{a}))
for all
\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)
. For any
a_{n+1}\in\widetilde{D}_{n+1}
put

\tilde{\mu}_{n+1}\left(J_{n+1}(\mathbf{a}a_{n+1})\right)\colon=\frac{1}{\#\widetilde{D}_{n+1}}\tilde{\mu}_{n}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right).

Since the measures
(\tilde{\mu}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are consistent, we apply the Daniell-Kolmogorov Consistency Theorem to obtain a probability measure
\tilde{\mu}
supported on
E
.

9.2.3.
Estimate of
\tilde{\mu}(J_{n}(\mathbf{a}))

Now, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\widetilde{D}_{n}
, we estimate
\tilde{\mu}(J_{n}(\mathbf{a}))
in terms of
|J_{n}(\mathbf{a})|
. Write
t_{0}\colon=\frac{2}{1+b}
and take any
0<t<t_{0}
.

Proposition 9.3
.

There exists some constant
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{2}(b,c)>0
such that for all large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)
we have

\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\leq\gamma_{2}|J_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{t}.

First, we estimate the number of elements in each
\#D_{n}
. Recall that
\#\{a\in\mathbb{Z}[i]:\|a\|\leq m\}=(2m+1)^{2}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 9.4
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\#\widetilde{D}_{k}\geq 8c^{2b^{M_{0}+k+1}}
.

Proof.

Take
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
d\in\mathscr{D}
. Then,
d\in\widetilde{D}_{k}
if and only if

\left\lceil c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}\right\rceil\leq\|d\|\leq\left\lfloor 2c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}\right\rfloor.

As a consequence, we have

\displaystyle\#\widetilde{D}_{k}

\displaystyle=\left(2\left\lfloor 2c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}\right\rfloor+1\right)^{2}-\left(2\left\lceil c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}-1\right\rceil+1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(4c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}-1\right)^{2}-\left(2c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}+1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=12c^{2b^{M_{0}+k+1}}\left(1-\frac{1}{c^{b^{M_{0}+k+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle>8c^{2b^{M_{0}+k+1}}.

\displaystyle(\text{by }M_{0}\geq\log_{b}\log_{c}3)

∎

Proposition 9.5
.

There exists some constant
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}(b,c)>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)
we have

(68)

\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\leq\frac{\gamma_{1}}{c^{tb^{M_{0}+n+1}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2t}}.

Proof.

Take
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\Pre(n)
. We obtain (
68
) by showing that

(69)

\log\left(\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\right)+\log\left(c^{tb^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2t}\right)

is bounded from above. We deal with each term of the sum separately. First, we show that

\log\left(\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\right)\leq-n\log 8-b^{M_{0}+n+1}\frac{2b}{b-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{b^{n}}\right)\log c.

This follows from

\displaystyle\log\left(\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{1}{\#\widetilde{D}_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{1}{8}\right)+\log\left(\frac{1}{c^{2b^{M_{0}+j+1}}}\right)

(by Lemma
9.4
)

\displaystyle=-n\log 8-2b^{M_{0}+2}\log c\sum_{j=1}^{n}b^{j-1}

\displaystyle=-n\log 8-2b^{M_{0}+2}\left(\frac{b^{n}-1}{b-1}\right)\log c

\displaystyle=-n\log 8-b^{M_{0}+n+1}\frac{2b}{b-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{b^{n}}\right)\log c.

Now, we prove that the second term in (
69
) verifies

\log\left(c^{tb^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2t}\right)\leq tb^{M_{0}+n+1}\log c\left(b+\frac{2b}{b-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{b^{n}}\right)\right)+5nt\log 2.

Indeed, from

|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(|a_{j}|+1\right)\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sqrt{2}\|a_{j}\|+1\right)\leq 8^{\frac{n}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|a_{j}\|,

we obtain

\log|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{n}{2}\log 8+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log\|a_{j}\|.

Thus,

\displaystyle\log\left(c^{tb^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq tb^{M_{0}+n+2}\log c+2t\left(\frac{n}{2}\log 8+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log\|a_{j}\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq tb^{M_{0}+n+2}\log c+3tn\log 2+2t\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log 2+b^{M_{0}+j+1}\log c\right)

\displaystyle=tbb^{M_{0}+n+1}\log c+5nt\log 2+2tb^{M_{0}+2}\left(\frac{b^{n}-1}{b-1}\right)\log c

\displaystyle=tb^{M_{0}+n+1}\log c\left(b+\frac{2b}{b-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{b^{n}}\right)\right)+5nt\log 2.

Combining the estimates, we can bound the sum (
69
) as follows:

\displaystyle\log

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right)\right)+\log\left(c^{tb^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2t}\right)

(70)

\displaystyle<b^{M_{0}+n+1}\log c\left(tb+\frac{2tb}{b-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{b^{n}}\right)-\frac{2b}{b-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{b^{n}}\right)\right)+n(5t\log 2-\log 8).

Using the identity

t_{0}b+\frac{2t_{0}b}{b-1}-\frac{2b}{b-1}=0,

we may see that the coefficient of
b^{M_{0}+n+1}
in (
70
) is negative for large
n
and the sum in (
69
) is bounded above.
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.3
.

Using the notation of Subsection
6.2
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\widetilde{D}_{n}
we have

J_{n}(\mathbf{a})=Q_{n}\left(\mathfrak{Treb}\left(\left(\left\lfloor 2c^{b^{M_{0}+n+1}}\right\rfloor+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1},\left(\left\lceil c^{b^{M_{0}+n+1}}\right\rceil-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}\right);\mathbf{a}\right).

Therefore, there are absolute constants for which

\left|J_{n}(\mathbf{a})\right|\asymp\frac{1}{c^{b^{M_{0}+n+1}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2}},

and the result follows from Proposition
9.5
.
∎

9.2.4.
Estimates of
\tilde{\mu}(\mathbb{D}(z;r))

Next, we estimate the measure of small disc. To this end, define

r_{0}\colon=\frac{1}{2}\min\{|J_{1}(a)|:a\in\widetilde{D}_{1}\}.

Proposition 9.6
.

There is a constant
\gamma_{3}=\gamma_{3}(b,c)>0
such that
\mu(\mathbb{D}(z;r))<\gamma_{3}r^{t}
whenever
z\in E
and
0<r<r_{0}
.

Proof.

Take any
z=[a_{1},a_{2},\ldots]\in E
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
0<r<r_{0}
. Let
n
be the single natural number verifying

|J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1})|<r\leq|J_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|.

Define

\widetilde{\gamma}\colon=\frac{1}{16\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}.

We consider two possible cases: either

(71)

|J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1})|<r\leq\widetilde{\gamma}\,|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1})|

or

(72)

\widetilde{\gamma}\,|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|<r\leq|J_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|.

Suppose that (
71
) is true. We claim that
\mathbb{D}(z;r)
intersects exactly one cylinder of level
n+1
, hence at most one set of the form
J_{n+1}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n+1})
. We prove this in two steps. First, we show that

(73)

\mathbb{D}\left(\frac{p_{n+1}(z)}{q_{n+1}(z)};\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|}{4\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}).

We afterward verify that

(74)

\mathbb{D}(z;\widetilde{\gamma}|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\left(\frac{p_{n+1}(z)}{q_{n+1}(z)};\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|}{4\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}\right).

The inclusion (
74
) follows directly from Proposition
6.4
and
\mathbb{D}(0;2^{-1})\subseteq\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}=\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
. To show (
73
), we note that the choice of
M_{0}
implies

\left|T^{n+1}(z)\right|<\frac{\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}{8\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)},

because

\left|\frac{1}{T^{n+1}(z)}\right|\geq\|a_{n+2}(z)\|-\left|T^{n+2}(z)\right|\geq c^{b^{M_{0}}}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}>\frac{8\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}{\kappa_{1}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}.

Therefore, using Proposition
4.3
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{p_{n+1}(z)}{q_{n+1}(z)}-z\right|

\displaystyle=\left|Q_{n+1}(0;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}))-Q_{n+1}(T^{n+1}(z);(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a_{n+1}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|T^{n+1}(z)|}{|q_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|^{2}\left|1+\frac{q_{n}}{q_{n+1}}T^{n+1}(z)\right|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|.

As a consequence, for all
w\in\mathbb{D}(z;\widetilde{\gamma}\;|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|)
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{p_{n+1}(z)}{q_{n+1}(z)}-w\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{p_{n+1}(z)}{q_{n+1}(z)}-z\right|+|z-w|

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{4\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)}|\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|.

This proves (
73
), hence,
D(z;r)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
. We may, thus, conclude that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\cap J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right)\leq\gamma_{2}|J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})|^{t}\leq\gamma_{2}r^{t}.

Let us now assume (
72
). Define

\tilde{\mathsf{N}}\colon=\#\left\{h\in\widetilde{D}_{n+1}:J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},h)\cap\mathbb{D}(z;r)\neq\varnothing\right\}.

In view of

\bigcup_{a\in\widetilde{D}_{n+1}}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(a)\subseteq\mathbb{D}\left(0;2^{-1}\right)\subseteq\mathfrak{F}^{\circ},

for each
a\in\widetilde{D}_{n+1}
we apply
Q_{n+1}(\,\cdot\,;(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a))
and obtain

J_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a)\subseteq Q_{n+1}\left(\mathbb{D}\left(0;2^{-1}\right);(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a)\right)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a).

Therefore, since
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a)
contains only one set of the form
J_{n+1}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n+1})
,

\tilde{\mathsf{N}}\leq\#\left\{a\in\widetilde{D}_{n+1}:Q_{n+1}\left(\mathbb{D}\left(0;2^{-1}\right);(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a)\right)\cap\mathbb{D}(z;r)\neq\varnothing\right\}.

Recall that
\mathfrak{m}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}
. Then, there is an absolute constant
\gamma_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\left(Q_{n+1}\left(\mathbb{D}\left(0;2^{-1}\right);(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{3}\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{1}{|q_{n+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a)|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{3}\frac{\pi}{36}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|^{4}|a|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{3}\frac{\pi}{144}\frac{1}{|q_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|^{4}c^{4b^{M_{0}+n+2}}},

which gives

\tilde{\mathsf{N}}\leq\frac{144}{\gamma_{3}}|q_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})|^{4}c^{4b^{M_{0}+n+2}}r^{2}.

From
0<t<1
we get
0<\frac{t}{2}<\frac{1}{2}
, hence

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}\left(\mathbb{D}(z;r)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\right)\min\left\{1,\tilde{\mathsf{N}}\frac{1}{\#\widetilde{D}_{n+1}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mu}(J_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\min\left\{1,\frac{144}{\gamma_{4}}c^{4b^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{4}r^{2}\frac{1}{8c^{2b^{M_{0}+n+2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\right)\min\left\{1,18c^{2b^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{4}r^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mu}\left(J_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\right)18^{\frac{t}{2}}c^{tb^{M_{0}+n+2}}|q_{n}(\mathbf{a})|^{2t}r^{t}

\displaystyle<18\gamma_{2}r^{t}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
9.1
. Lower bound.

The Mass Distribution Principle and Proposition
9.6
imply (
67
). Since
E\subseteq\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)
, the lower bound in (
65
) follows.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

A slight modification of the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
9.1
tells us that for every
b,c>1
and
\delta>0
we have

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c)\setminus\widetilde{\Xi}(b,c+\delta)\right)=\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(\Xi(b,c)\setminus\Xi(b,c+\delta)\right)=\frac{2}{b+1}.

10.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Recall that

E_{\infty}(\Phi)\colon=\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq\Phi(n)\text{ \rm for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

i.

Assume that
B=1
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary and consider the infinite set

\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}\colon=\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Phi(n)\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{n}\right\}.

Then, we have

\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}\|\geq(1+\varepsilon)^{n}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathscr{L}\right\}\subseteq E_{\infty}(\Phi),

so
s_{1+\varepsilon}\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))
, by Corollary
8.24
. In view of Proposition
8.12
, we conclude that

2=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}s_{1+\varepsilon}\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))\leq 2.

ii.

Assume that
1<B<\infty
. For each
\varepsilon>0
, define the infinite set

\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\colon=\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Phi(n)\leq(B+\varepsilon)^{n}\right\}.

Hence,

\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}\|\geq(B+\varepsilon)^{n}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathscr{L}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right\}\subseteq E_{\infty}(\Phi)

and

E_{\infty}(\Phi)\subseteq\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}\|\geq(B-\varepsilon)^{n}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Therefore,
s_{B-\varepsilon}\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))\leq s_{B+\varepsilon}
and, since
B\mapsto s_{B}
is continuous (Proposition
8.12
),
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\infty}(\Phi))=s_{B}
.

iii.

Assume that
B=\infty
. Further, assume that
b=1
. Then, given
\varepsilon>0
, the set

\mathscr{L}\colon=\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Phi(n)\leq e^{(1+\varepsilon)^{n}}\right\}

is infinite and we have

\displaystyle\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq e^{(1+\varepsilon)^{n}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq e^{(1+\varepsilon)^{n}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathscr{L}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq E_{\infty}(\Phi),

so, Theorem
9.1
gives

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)\geq\frac{2}{2+\varepsilon}.

Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we conclude
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)\geq 1
. Moreover,
B=\infty
implies that, for any
C>1
, the inequality
\frac{\log\Phi(n)}{n}\geq\log C
holds for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
, hence

E_{\infty}(\Phi)\subseteq\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:C^{n}\leq\|a_{n}(z)\|\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

and
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)\leq s_{C}
. We let
C\to\infty
and recourse to Lemma
8.12
to conclude
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)\leq 1
, so

\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)=1.

Suppose that
1<b<\infty
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, the inequality
\Phi(n)\leq e^{(b+\varepsilon)^{n}}
holds infinitely often while
\Phi(n)\geq e^{(b-\varepsilon)^{n}}
is true for every large
n
. As a consequence, since the set

\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq e^{(b+\varepsilon)^{n}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

is contained in

\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq e^{(b+\varepsilon)^{n}}\geq\Phi(n)\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subseteq E_{\infty}(\Phi)

and

E_{\infty}(\Phi)\subseteq\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq\Phi(n)\geq e^{(b-\varepsilon)^{n}}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

Theorem
9.1
tells us that

\frac{2}{b+1+\varepsilon}\leq\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)\leq\frac{2}{b+1-\varepsilon}.

Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we arrive at
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)=\frac{2}{b+1}
.

To finish the proof, assume that
b=\infty
. Then,
\Phi(n)\geq e^{c^{n}}
for all
c>0
and every sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
; that is,

E_{\infty}(\Phi)\subseteq\left\{z\in\mathfrak{F}:\|a_{n}(z)\|\geq e^{c^{n}}\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Theorem
9.1
yields
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)\leq\frac{2}{1+c}
and, taking
c\to\infty
, we conclude that
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}\left(E_{\infty}(\Phi)\right)=0
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

11.
Concluding remarks and open problems

In the recent literature, many new metrical results (including zero-one laws and computation of
Hausdorff dimensions) on sets of real numbers
x
defined in terms
of their partial quotients
a_{n}(x)
and/or of their convergents
p_{n}(x)/q_{n}(x)
have been established.
We survey some of them below and briefly discuss their complex analogs, where regular continued fractions of real numbers are replaced by Hurwitz continued fractions of complex numbers.

As discussed in the introduction, stemming from Borel-Berstein and Wang-Wu theorems, the focus is on the growth properties of partial quotients. To this end, Sun and Wu
[
54
]
established that, for any
b>0
, the Hausdorff dimension of the level set

\Bigl\{x\in(0,1):\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\log a_{n+1}(x)}{\log q_{n}(x)}=b\Bigr\}

is equal to
1/(b+2)
. A localized version (where
b
is replaced by a continuous function of
x
) has subsequently
been proved in
[
10
]
, see
[
57
]
for a further extension.
Their complex analogs should hold. To establish it,
one first needs to prove the complex analog of
[
21
, Lemma 3.2]
. Specifically, in
[
21
]
, the authors calculated the Hausdorff dimension of the level set

E(\alpha)=\left\{x\in[0,1):\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log a_{1}(x)+\cdots+\log a_{n}(x)}{n}=\alpha\right\},

for any given
\alpha>0
. They proved, quite unexpectedly, that the graph of the function
\alpha\mapsto\dim E(\alpha)

is neither convex nor concave.
Later, Fan et al.
[
19
]

considered the subsets of
E(\infty)
defined by

E(\psi,\alpha)=\left\{x\in[0,1):\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log a_{1}(x)+\cdots+\log a_{n}(x)}{\psi(n)}=\alpha\right\}\text{ for all }\alpha>0,

where
\psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is such that
\psi(n)/n
tends to
\infty
with
n
.
They obtained the Hausdorff dimension of
E(\psi,\alpha)
. Subsequently, Liao and Rams
[
46
]

computed the Hausdorff dimension of sets defined as
E(\psi,\alpha)
, but with
the limit replaced by
\liminf
(resp., by
\limsup
)
and thereby exhibited a new phenomenon.
It would be desirable to establish the analogs of all these results for Hurwitz continued fractions and to
determine whether the unexpected phenomena also occur in this setting.

Very recently, He and Xiao
[
28
]
proved results concerning
the sets of complex numbers defined by the
growth rate of products of consecutive partial quotients. Thereby they obtained complex analogs of results
established for real continued fractions in
[
35
]
. They observe that, in order to extend
their Theorem 1.3 to functions that are not continuous, the auxiliary results on the geometry of irregular cylinders
established in the present paper may be useful.

It is worth mentioning that the study of consecutive partial quotients gives information regarding the improbability (or not) of Dirichlet’s theorem, see
[
4
,
37
,
42
]
. However, interestingly products of partial quotients arising from the arithmetic progressions give information regarding the set of exceptions to the convergence criterion of Host-Kra (2005) and Bourgain (1989) of multiple ergodic averages for the Gauss map
[
38
]
. Establishing analogs of these results for Hcfs is possible by using the geometric properties proved in this paper, along with some other ideas.

There is a vast literature on the determination of the
Hausdorff dimension of sets of real numbers whose partial quotients are all in a given finite (or infinite) set; see
e.g.
[
15
,
32
]
and the references given therein.
Getting a complex analog seems to be a challenging problem because the symbolic space associated to Hcfs cannot be modelled as a countable Markov shift.

Chevallier
[
12
]
used flows on spaces of lattices in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
to prove the analog of Dirichlet’s theorem for
approximation in
\mathbb{Q}({i})
. Let
c(z)
denote the smallest real number such that, for every real number
Q>1
, there exist
Gaussian integers
p,q
such that

0<|q|<Q,\quad|qz-p|\leq\frac{c(z)}{Q}.

He proved that
c(z)\leq\sqrt{2}/(3-\sqrt{3})=1.115\ldots
, with equality for almost all
z

(in the sense of the Lebesgue measure).
It would be interesting to find an alternative proof, in the spirit of
Davenport and Schmidt
[
16
]
, which may also give an explicit
formula to compute
c(z)
in terms of the Hurwitz continued fraction of
z
.
Another related question is the determination of the smallest possible constant
c(z)
.
In the real case, this corresponds to the constant associated with the Golden Ratio
[
16
]
.

We finalize the paper with a simple question motivated by theorems
1.4
and
1.5
. Let
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary function. We have defined

E(\Phi)\colon=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|a_{n}(z)|\geq\Phi(n)\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Recall that for any
x\in(0,1)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
we denote
n
-th partial quotient of the regular continued fraction of
x
as
A_{n}(x)
. Define the set

E_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)\colon=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|A_{n}(x)|\geq\Phi(n)\text{ for infinitely many }n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
tell us that
\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E(\Phi))=2\dim_{\operatorname{H}}(E_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi))
holds whenever
\Phi
has a subsequence of very fast growth. Whether it would hold for any
\Phi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is a natural open problem.
